In fact the conditional moment at any even \(m>0\) in this sequence is outside the range of the conditional projection even as an individual polynomial.

Its compact integral is zero by orthogonality. Its integral over \(1<w<3\) is strictly positive at even \(m\) because \(g(w^2)^m\ge0\) and \(\mathcal L_{2bm}(w)>0\) for \(w>1\). The latter follows, for example, from the real-root theorem for orthogonal polynomials on \([-1,1]\) and their positive leading coefficients. This contradicts the range identity. The conclusion over real closed fields follows by specialization of the finitely many coefficients to an ordered subfield of \(\mathbb R\), using the rational conditional and integration formulas.


## Finite rotation stabilizers of classical sign twists

**Theorem (The diagonal block test for finite toral stabilizers).**

Use the classical sign torsor \(X\), scalar dimension \(e\), power-of-two negative rank \(r\), and subgroup \(S\) from the diagonal block theorem. Suppose \(K(i)\) is a field and write \(U_1\) for its norm-one torus. Let \(A\) be a finite source subgroup acting in a source sign basis as follows. In the orthogonal case it acts by \(U_1\)-characters on definite two-planes \(\pm I_2\) and fixes the remaining lines. In the special unitary and quaternionic cases it acts by \(U_1\)-characters on signed lines, using the fixed complex subfield in the quaternionic case. Assume that \(A\) meets the geometric center of the source group trivially.

For each nontrivial character \(\chi:A\to U_1\), let \(a_\chi\) be the signed rank of the blocks carrying \(\chi\), with a two-plane counted as two orthogonal coordinates. Suppose \(r\mid a_\chi\) for each such character and
\[
           \prod_{\chi\ne 1}\chi^{a_\chi/r}
                \ \text{is a square in }\operatorname{Hom}_K(A,U_1).
\]
Then \(K(X/A)^S\) has level \(\min(s(K),er)\) and \(X/A\) has a regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample. These conclusions pass to any finite source subgroup containing \(A\) with odd geometric index.

To see this, put \(F=K(X/A)^S\). The generic geometric orbit of \(S\times A\) on \(X\) is free by the conjugacy argument in the block theorem, now applied to each geometric element of \(A\). The projection of the cocycle to \(A\), and then along each character to \(U_1\), gives a class
\[
              [\delta_\chi]\in F^\times/N_{F(i)/F}F(i)^\times
                       =H^1(F,U_1).
\]
Use the split étale norm if \(F(i)\) splits. Twisting a unit complex line by this cocycle gives the Hermitian line with coefficient \(\delta_\chi\): its norm-one frame torsor is the norm equation with that right side, up to inverting the coefficient, which gives the same norm class. The orthogonal two-plane is the scalar form of that line. In the quaternionic case the line quaternionifies to the signed quaternionic line with this coefficient because the original circle action is left multiplication through the complex subfield. Hence the comparison of scalar forms from the block proof has source correction
\[
                      (e\cdot1)\sum_{\chi\ne1}
                               a_\chi(\langle\delta_\chi\rangle-1).
\]
In the orthogonal case a change of a norm representative preserves the two-plane scalar form. The same holds for the higher scalar dimensions in the other cases.

Let \(u=\log_2(er)\) and set \(\eta=\sum_{\chi\ne1}(a_\chi/r)(\langle\delta_\chi\rangle-1)\). Its signed discriminant belongs to the norm group. Indeed, the product of the norm classes is the class induced by the character product in the hypothesis, which is trivial since the norm quotient has exponent two. Thus \((2\cdot1)\eta\in I^3\) by the binary Pfister argument of the block theorem. If \(er\ge2\), the correction \((er\cdot1)\eta\) belongs to \(I^{u+2}\). This covers all of the stated cases, including \(r=1,e=4\). If \(s(F)>er\), the target comparison puts \(2er\cdot1\) in \(I^{u+2}\), contradicting the Hauptsatz as before. The matching level lower bound follows from the same point over \(K(S_{er}^-)\), or over \(K\) if \(s(K)\le er\). The standard form of \(S\) is isotropic at this level and the finite stabilizer makes its split rank-one action active on the generic fibre. Apply the generic fibre theorem and odd-index descent as in the block case.

For example, over a real closed field take the rank-ten quaternionic torsor of negative rank two. For every integer \(k\ge2\) let \(A\) be cyclic of order \(2^k\), with a faithful character \(\chi\) into \(U_1\). Give four positive lines character \(\chi\), the two negative lines character \(\chi^2\), and the four remaining positive lines the trivial character. The signed ranks \(4,-2\) satisfy the theorem with character product \(\chi^2(\chi^2)^{-1}=1\). The only nontrivial real cocycle of this constant cyclic group is its involution. It flips precisely the four positive lines of character \(\chi\), so the two possible source signatures have negative ranks two and six. Thus \(X/A\) has no rational point. The characters \(\chi,\chi^2,1\) are distinct even up to inversion, and all their spaces are definite. The source centralizer therefore has compact identity component. These quotients give regular counterexamples with arbitrary powers of two as the orders of the finite stabilizers.


**Corollary (Constant cyclic stabilizers over a real closed field).**

Keep the rank bounds of the diagonal block theorem for the classical sign torsor \(X\) over a real closed field \(K\). Let \(A\) be a constant finite cyclic source subgroup of even order, and let \(h\) be its unique involution. If the signed rank \(a(h)\) of its minus eigenspace satisfies \(a(h)\not\equiv r\bmod 2r\), then \(X/A\) has a regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample. The invariant field for the subgroup described below has level \(er\). The same conclusions hold on \(X/H\) for any finite \(H\) containing \(A\) with odd geometric index. The quotient \(X/A\) has a \(K\)-point exactly when \(a(h)=-r\).

It suffices to pass to the two-primary part of \(A\) and use odd-index descent. Choose block size \(d=r+1\) in the orthogonal and special unitary cases, and \(d=2r+1\) in the quaternionic case. The proofs of the block and toral level bounds only need these lower bounds and \(rd\le n\). This choice makes \(d\) odd, so the acting diagonal group \(S\) contains no nonidentity central two-element of the target in the three cases. In the unitary case, a scalar in \(S\) when the fixed complement is zero has order dividing \(d\), which is odd. Thus the generic action of \(S\times A\) is free even if \(A\) meets the source center. We may use the cocycle comparison from the toral proof. For \(|A|=2\), the involution calculation already gives the claim with this \(d\).

Suppose \(|A|=2^k\) with \(k\ge2\). A finite cyclic action preserves a positive-negative decomposition of the source. To obtain it, average a positive definite form over the finite group, use the self-adjoint operator representing the indefinite form with respect to that positive one, and separate its positive and negative eigenspaces. Diagonalize the action on the definite summands into circle characters in the Hermitian cases and into real rotation planes and sign lines in the orthogonal case. This uses the spectral theorem for an isometry of a definite form, which over a real closed field follows from diagonalization over \(K(i)\); in the quaternionic case, complex conjugate eigenvalues merge into quaternionic lines. All circle characters are powers of a faithful \(\chi:A\to U_1\). The \(U_1\)-torsor class induced by the source cocycle along \(\chi^j\) equals \([\delta]^j\) in the norm quotient. It is therefore trivial for even \(j\) and \([\delta]\) for odd \(j\). The signed rank of the odd-exponent blocks is exactly \(a(h)\), including both coordinates in each orthogonal plane.

In the orthogonal case, the nontrivial sign lines are acted on by the sign character of \(A\) and are fixed by \(h\), since \(k\ge2\). Let \(\varepsilon\) be the square class of the induced sign cocycle. Its image in the norm quotient agrees with the class induced by \(\chi^{2^{k-1}}\), which is trivial. Hence \(\varepsilon\) is a norm. The number of sign lines is even because the source group is special orthogonal. Their combined scalar Witt correction is zero: their signed rank is even and \(2\langle1\rangle(\langle\varepsilon\rangle-1)=0\) by roundness. Thus in every case the total source correction is
\[
                      a(h)(e\cdot1)(\langle\delta\rangle-1).
\]
The two cases of the single-involution Witt comparison now give level \(er\), and the resulting split rank-one action yields the regular witness. The rational point assertion uses the two quadratic cocycles of the original constant cyclic group, indexed by \(1\) and \(h\), with negative ranks \(r\) and \(r+a(h)\).


## Continued periods for the long-root and fifteen-mirror heights

**Theorem (A second period for each mirror-circle trace).**

Let \(F(v)\) be the angular conditional polynomial of a regular function on the compact quotient, after applying the left-circle Reynolds projection if necessary. On the octahedral long-root chart (H15), and on the icosahedral fifteen-mirror chart, respectively, one has
\[
        \int_1^3 F(w^2)\,dw=2\int_0^1 F(w^2)\,dw,\qquad
        \int_1^5 F(w^2)\,dw=4\int_0^1 F(w^2)\,dw.
\]
The identities hold coefficientwise for a regular Laurent function, including any marked power. For either chart, and any \(b\ge1\) and nonzero real polynomial \(g\) with \(\deg g<b\), the conditional polynomial \(g(v)^m\mathcal L_{2bm}(\sqrt v)\) is outside the regular range at every positive even \(m\). Together with the coordinate-mirror range theorem, this excludes the powered Legendre realization on three of the exceptional height charts.

We use the following algebraic fact about the angular quotient. Let \(\mathcal Q\) be the complex unit sphere, let \(\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SO}(3)\) be the rotation group, and let \(\Omega\) be its invariant algebraic area form. For any \(\Gamma\)-invariant regular function \(f\), with compact mean \(\lambda(f)\), the form \((f-\lambda(f))\Omega\) is the exterior derivative of a regular \(\Gamma\)-invariant one-form on \(\mathcal Q\). Indeed, each positive-degree spherical harmonic \(h\) of degree \(j\) has \(\Delta h=-j(j+1)h\), so a primitive for \(h\Omega\) is \(-\iota_{\nabla_{\mathcal Q}h}\Omega/[j(j+1)]\). The spherical gradient is regular on \(\mathcal Q\). Decompose the polynomial into spherical harmonics and average the primitive under \(\Gamma\).

For any dominant algebraic map from an irreducible surface \(T\) to \(\mathcal Q/\Gamma\), the invariant primitive pulls back as a regular form on the smooth locus of \(T\). Here is a local verification. Choose a generic lift to \(\mathcal Q\) over a finite extension of the function field of \(T\) and normalize \(T\) in that extension. The normalization maps to \(\mathcal Q\) by the finite fibre product, so the pulled-back primitive is regular at its codimension-one points. At a divisor of the smooth locus of \(T\) a genuine pole of a rational one-form cannot disappear under finite pullback in characteristic zero. To see this, use a basis consisting of \(dt/t\) and regular tangential differentials at a uniformizer \(t\). In the extension \(t=s^e a\), with \(a\) a unit, \(dt/t=e\,ds/s+da/a\). The map on leading logarithmic coefficients is injective, by separability on tangential differentials and \(e\ne0\) on the normal coefficient. Thus a negative order in this logarithmic basis remains negative, and a logarithmic residue of order zero remains nonzero. Regularity at every divisor gives regularity on the smooth locus. Consequently on any closed smooth oriented real two-cycle in that locus, the pullback of \(f\Omega\) has integral equal to \(\lambda(f)\) times the integral of the pulled-back area form, by Stokes' theorem. The pullback identities initially computed at generic points extend across the cycle.

For (H15) take \(T\) to be the smooth surface
\[
                 u^2+y^2=(1-w^2)(9-w^2).
\]
Set \(v=w^2\), \(a=3-v+u\), and use \(p,q,r,k\) from (H15). The signed angular invariant can be taken as \(J=wary/216\). In fact \(q=ay^2/864\), \(k=ra/6\), and the angular cubic discriminant is \(f=vrk/9\), giving \(J^2=qf\). These polynomials define a dominant map to the rotation quotient, since the inverse rational height and the formula for \(u\) recover \(v,u\) from the reflection invariants. The pulled-back area form is a fixed nonzero scalar times \(dw\wedge du/y\). This follows from the physical conditional density (H15) with the orientation retained, using \(u=\sqrt{(1-v)(9-v)}\cos\theta\), \(y=\sqrt{(1-v)(9-v)}\sin\theta\). On a physical open patch the absolute density identifies the ratio of the two real forms up to a constant sign. The ratio is rational, and the patch is Zariski dense, so the signed identity holds algebraically.

Use the closed real two-cycle on this complex surface with \(1\le w\le3\), and \(u=i\rho\cos\theta,\ y=i\rho\sin\theta\), where \(\rho=\sqrt{(w^2-1)(9-w^2)}\). Both endpoint circles collapse smoothly, since the zeros of \(\rho^2\) are simple. In these coordinates the form \(dw\wedge du/y\) is \(-dw\wedge d\theta\). The angular average of the pullback of \(f\) on the continued circle equals \(F(v)\): expanding in \(u,y\), the circle average of every monomial is a polynomial in \((1-v)(9-v)\), with odd exponents integrating to zero. Hence the normalized period on the cycle is \(\frac12\int_1^3 F(w^2)\,dw\). The algebraic primitive argument identifies it with the physical mean \(\int_0^1 F(w^2)\,dw\).

For the fifteen-mirror chart start with the surface \(u^2+y^2=(1-w^2)(25-w^2)\) and set
\[
 v=w^2,\quad d=v^2-22v+45,\quad
 X=\frac{d+(9-v)u}{90},\qquad Y=\frac{(9-v)y}{2\alpha},\quad \alpha=3\sqrt5 .
\]
Take the double cover \(\xi^2=X\). The mirror formulas
\[
       p=((v-3)X+\xi Y)/24,\quad
       r=3p(1-X)-X^2+X-16vX/45,\quad
       J=4w(45p^2+13p+1-5r)\xi/\alpha
\]
define the dominant map to the oriented angular quotient. The inverse rational mirror height and the signed chart formulas recover the source coordinates generically up to finite choices. The polynomial relations hold by the physical chart, and this double cover is irreducible because \(X\) has simple zeros, as also seen on the cycle below. The pulled-back area form is a fixed nonzero scalar times the pullback of \(dw\wedge du/y\). As in the octahedral case, the real conditional density, now uniform in \(w,\theta\) and in the sign of \(\xi\), proves this algebraically on a physical open patch.

Take \(1\le w\le5\) with \(u=i\rho\cos\theta,\ y=i\rho\sin\theta\) and \(\rho=\sqrt{(w^2-1)(25-w^2)}\) on the base. Lift the whole cycle to the double cover. The only branch points are \(v=11\pm2\sqrt{19}\), \(\cos\theta=0\), where the real and imaginary parts of \(X\) are transverse. At such a point \(d'(v)\ne0\), \(9-v\ne0\) and \(\rho\ne0\), so this follows by differentiating in \(w,\theta\). Locally the lifted cycle is the oriented simple branched cover \(\xi\mapsto\xi^2\) of a disk. Thus the lifted cycle is compact, closed, and smooth in the smooth locus of the algebraic surface. At the base endpoints \(X\) is nonzero, so there is no branching there. The normalized period on the lift is \(\frac14\int_1^5 F(w^2)\,dw\). Indeed the mean over the two signs of \(\xi\) reduces the integrand to a polynomial in \(X,Y,v,w\). Its circle averages are polynomial in \(w\) by the same expansion as on the physical chart, where they equal \(F(w^2)\). The algebraic primitive again proves equality with the physical mean.

The period identities for the conditional polynomials extend to real closed fields by their rational integration formulas and specialization of finitely many coefficients. Finally, \(g(v)^m\mathcal L_{2bm}(\sqrt v)\) integrates to zero on the physical interval by Legendre orthogonality, and to a strictly positive number on the continued interval at even \(m\), by the same argument as for the coordinate mirrors. This proves the range obstruction.


## Many simultaneous rotations in a finite abelian stabilizer

**Theorem (A signed-rank criterion for abelian stabilizers).**

For a classical sign torsor as in the block theorem over a real closed \(K\), let \(A\) be a constant finite abelian source subgroup and set \(V=A[2]\). For an element \(h\in V\), let \(a(h)\) denote its signed negative-eigenspace rank. Assume either that the source type is special unitary or quaternionic, that \(A\) is elementary abelian, or that in the orthogonal case \(A\) acts on definite rotation planes and fixed lines as in the finite toral theorem. Then \(X/A\) has a regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample in either of the following cases.

- For every \(h\in V\), \(a(h)\equiv0\bmod 2r\).
- For \(r=2\), for every \(h\in V\), \(a(h)\not\equiv2\bmod4\).

The diagonal quotient field used for these conclusions has level \(er\). Both conclusions descend to a finite source supergroup of odd geometric index. The criterion imposes congruences on the signed ranks of the involutions without a divisibility hypothesis on the separate character multiplicities.

Pass to the two-primary subgroup of \(A\) and take the smallest permitted block size as in the constant cyclic argument. This removes the geometric central kernel of the generic \(S\times A\) action. In the unitary and quaternionic cases decompose into circle characters on signed lines. A common definite decomposition is obtained using an invariant positive form, and the commuting definite isometries diagonalize simultaneously over \(K(i)\). For quaternionic representations this can be seen in the defining complex representation: its simultaneous eigenspaces are paired under the quaternionic structure by complex conjugation, and on a real-character eigenspace one chooses a quaternionic orthonormal basis. In the orthogonal toral case use its given rotation planes. Because \(A\) is finite abelian, two circle characters have the same restriction to \(V\) exactly when their ratio is a square. Hence they induce the same class in \(F^\times/N F(i)^\times\), where \(F\) is the generic invariant field. The source correction may use any square-class representatives of those norm classes. Changing a representative does not change the block scalar Witt class, since the scalar dimension per block is a multiple of two. For elementary abelian sign actions use the induced square classes themselves.

Choose a basis of \(V\) and the dual sign characters, with representative scalars \(\delta_1,\ldots,\delta_l\) for their induced classes. Write \(d_j=\langle\delta_j\rangle-1\) in the Witt ring. A character corresponds to a subset \(C\) of the dual basis, and its induced form may be represented by \(\langle\prod_{j\in C}\delta_j\rangle\). Let \(a_C\) be the signed rank of all the blocks with that character restriction. The correction to the Witt identity is \((e\cdot1)\eta\) where
\[
           \eta=\sum_{\emptyset\ne J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,l\}}
                       A_J\prod_{j\in J} d_j,\qquad
           A_J=\sum_{C\supseteq J}a_C .
\]
For the involution with basis support \(I\), its signed rank is
\[
            a(h_I)=\sum_{\emptyset\ne J\subseteq I}
                                   (-2)^{|J|-1}A_J.
\]
This follows by expanding the indicator that a character has odd intersection with \(I\).

Write \(b=\log_2 r\), \(u=\log_2(er)\). If all \(a(h_I)\) are divisible by \(2r\), subset inversion shows that \(2^{|J|-1}A_J\) is divisible by \(2r\). Each summand of \(\eta\) therefore lies in \(I_{\mathrm W}^{b+2}\), where \(I_{\mathrm W}\) is the fundamental ideal: for \(|J|\le b+2\), its coefficient is divisible by \(2^{b+2-|J|}\), and larger \(|J|\) already give the required power. Consequently the correction lies in \(I_{\mathrm W}^{u+2}\). The block theorem comparison gives \(s(F)=er\).

For \(r=2\) suppose no \(a(h)\equiv2\bmod4\). The parity \(a(h)\bmod2\) is a linear functional on \(V\). If it vanishes identically, use the first case. Otherwise take the basis so it is the first coordinate. Then \(A_{\{1\}}\) is odd, \(4\mid A_{\{j\}}\) for \(j>1\), and \(2\mid A_{\{i,j\}}\) when \(i,j>1\) by applying the signed-rank formula to \(h_i+h_j\). Modulo \(I_{\mathrm W}^3\), \(\eta\) is therefore \(d_1 B\) with \(B\) of odd rank. The scalar comparison in the block proof puts \((e\cdot1)\eta\) in \(I_{\mathrm W}^{u+1}\), since the target term belongs to \(I_{\mathrm W}^{u+2}\) and the original signature difference belongs to \(I_{\mathrm W}^{u+1}\). Reducing modulo \(I_{\mathrm W}^{u+1}\) now puts \((e\cdot1)d_1\) in that ideal. This form is similar to a Pfister form of dimension \(2e=2^u<2^{u+1}\), so it is hyperbolic by the Hauptsatz. Thus \((e\cdot1)\eta\in I_{\mathrm W}^{u+2}\) also in the second case. The same comparison gives the level, and the generic split action and odd descent give the regular witness.

For instance, in quaternionic rank ten, use \(A=(C_4)^2\) and give the three nontrivial sign characters on \(V=(C_2)^2\) signed ranks \(1,3,-2\), using any lifts to circle characters on \(A\). The four remaining positive coordinates have trivial character. The three nontrivial involutions have signed ranks \(4,-1,1\), so this meets the second criterion. The only possible negative ranks of the source twists are \(2,6,1,3\), all positive. The quotient is point-free. The character restrictions separate the three occupied nontrivial character spaces, each of which is definite, so the commuting identity component is compact.


## Finite nonabelian stabilizers in classical sign twists

**Theorem (A representation criterion for finite stabilizers).**

Use the classical sign torsor \(X\) of negative rank \(r\) from the diagonal block theorem, over a real closed field \(K\), and its rank bounds. Let \(H\) be a constant finite subgroup of the source group and \(A\) a Sylow two-subgroup. Choose an \(A\)-invariant definite positive-negative decomposition. For each isometry type \(\rho\) of an irreducible definite \(A\)-module over the coefficient algebra, define \(m_\rho\) to be its positive multiplicity minus its negative multiplicity. The trivial module may have arbitrary signed multiplicity. Then \(X/H\) has a regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample, and the invariant field used below has level \(er\), under either of the following hypotheses.

- Every nontrivial \(m_\rho\) is divisible by \(2r\). This includes quaternionic \(A\)-modules without a preserved complex plane.
- In the orthogonal or special unitary cases, every nontrivial \(m_\rho\) is divisible by \(r\), and the determinant character \(\prod_{\rho\ne1}\det(\rho)^{m_\rho/r}\) is trivial in the orthogonal case or is a square as a norm-one character in the unitary case. In the quaternionic case the same assertion holds when the positive and negative modules are obtained from complex Hermitian \(A\)-modules by quaternionification, using the complex irreducibles and their norm-one determinant characters for this criterion.

Over an arbitrary characteristic-zero field the same criterion works given the indicated signed isometric block decomposition for a finite subgroup \(A\) of odd geometric index in \(H\), provided no nonidentity central element of \(A\) is transported to \(S\) by a geometric isometry. The exact level in that case is \(\min(s(K),er)\).

Over \(K\) real closed, the invariant definite decomposition can be constructed by averaging a positive definite form over \(A\) and taking its positive and negative spectral spaces for the source form. Definite \(A\)-modules split into orthogonal irreducibles. Two invariant positive definite forms on an isomorphic module are equivariantly isometric: the positive self-adjoint operator comparing them has a positive square root commuting with \(A\). The same argument applies to the negative form after reversing its sign.

Use the smallest allowed diagonal block size \(d=r+1\) for \(e=1,2\) and \(d=2r+1\) for \(e=4\). The field \(F=K(X/A)^S\) has a free generic geometric action of \(S\times A\) on the torsor, as in the block theorem. The choice of odd \(d\) excludes nonidentity central two-elements in \(S\), and the arbitrary-field hypothesis gives the same conclusion there. Project the generic orbit cocycle to \(A\). For an irreducible template \(\rho\), twist its positive standard form by that cocycle and diagonalize over the coefficient algebra. Write \(\beta_\rho\) for the diagonal quadratic coefficient class minus \(\dim(\rho)\langle1\rangle\), using the dimension over the coefficient algebra of the template. In the quaternionification hypothesis use the twisted complex template. Its diagonal coefficients are also those of the quaternionified form. Thus \(\beta_\rho\) has even rank, and the correction to the scalar Witt comparison is
\[
                         (e\cdot1)\sum_{\rho\ne1}m_\rho\beta_\rho.
\]
This calculation may be made under the contradictory assumption \(s(F)>er\), where the Hermitian coefficient algebra is division. In the first case the correction belongs to \(I^{\log_2(er)+2}\), since each \(\beta_\rho\in I\) and every \((e\cdot1)m_\rho\) is an integer multiple of \(2er\cdot1\).

For the determinant case put \(\beta=\sum_{\rho\ne1}(m_\rho/r)\beta_\rho\). In the orthogonal case the signed discriminant of each \(\beta_\rho\) is the square class induced by the determinant character of the twisted representation, since taking determinants of the descended Gram matrix gives precisely the associated quadratic cocycle. In the complex Hermitian case the same Gram calculation gives the induced class in the norm quotient. Products for the virtual signed multiplicities agree with the determinant character in the hypothesis. Hence \(\beta\in I^2\) in the orthogonal case, and \((2\cdot1)\beta\in I^3\) in the complex cases by the binary Pfister argument in the block theorem. The correction \((er\cdot1)\beta\) again belongs to \(I^{\log_2(er)+2}\).

The scalar comparison and Hauptsatz now give the level upper bound as in the block theorem. The source and target become isometric over a field of level \(\min(s(K),er)\), so the same smooth-point argument gives equality. Isotropy of the standard form of \(S\) gives an active split rank-one action on the generic fibre. The regular witness and odd-degree descent to \(H\) follow from the generic-fibre theorem and Springer.

For example, use quaternionic rank three and negative rank one. Let \(H\) be \(2O\) or \(2I\), or any nontrivial finite subgroup of the Hamilton norm-one group. Let it act by left multiplication on each of the two positive quaternionic lines and trivially on the negative line. The positive line module is irreducible over the quaternions and has no trivial submodule. Its signed multiplicity is two, so the first criterion applies. The same construction in rank ten with eight positive lines and two negative lines works for \(r=2\). Over \(K\) real closed, a rational point on \(X/H\) would come from twisting \(X\) by an element of order dividing two in \(H\). Every such involution fixes the negative subspace, so each twisted source still has a nonzero negative index. The quotient is point-free. Its commuting identity component is compact because every isotypic space of the \(H\)-action is definite. This gives regular examples with nonabelian stabilizers, including both binary polyhedral groups.


## Real branch contacts for regular Laurent trinomials

**Theorem (The zero-product levels bound all real branch points).**

Let \(M\) be the unit two-sphere or three-sphere with normalized area, and let \(P=A+B/t+Ct\) have real polynomial coefficients on \(M\). Put \(G=-4BC\) and assume \(G\not\equiv0\). If the pure moments vanish eventually, the zero set \(Z=\{G=0\}\) is nonempty, and with \(b_+=\max_Z A\), \(b_-=\min_Z A\) one has
\[
 \begin{gathered}
                         b_-<0<b_+,\\
              b_-\le A-\sqrt{-G}\le A+\sqrt{-G}\le b_+
                                    \quad\text{where }G\le0 .
 \end{gathered}
\]
At every extremal contact on \(Z\), \(dG=0\) and the tangent Hessian of \(G\) is positive semidefinite. At each extremal level there must be at least one contact where \(G\) is not locally the square of a smooth function with nonzero differential at the contact. In dimension two, a contact at either extremal level with \(dA=0\) and \(G=O(|x|^4)\) in local coordinates already excludes eventual vanishing.

The theorem applies after lifting regular angular phases on either exceptional product to \(S^2\), and after lifting full regular phases to \(S^3\). In particular it rules out trinomials with disjoint regular zero sets for \(B\) and \(C\), as well as trinomials whose simultaneous zeros are transverse and whose other zeros are regular. For \(G=R^2\) an angular trinomial that survives must have, at each extremal contact level, some contact with \(dR=0\) and \(dA\ne0\).

For large positive \(z\), the scalar Cauchy transform is
\[
                    J(z)=\int_M\frac{d\mu}{\sqrt{(z-A)^2+G}},
\]
with the positive square root. Eventual vanishing would make \(J(z)\) a polynomial in \(1/z\) with coefficient one at \(1/z\). If \(Z\) is empty, \(G\) has a strict sign by connectedness. For \(G>0\) the integral is analytic at zero. For \(G<0\) every term in the scalar constant term of an even power is nonnegative and the contribution of \((BC)^m\) at order \(2m\) is strictly positive. Thus assume \(Z\ne\varnothing\) and put \(c_*=\max_{\{G\le0\}}(A+\sqrt{-G})\). The Cauchy identity continues on the interval \(z>\max(c_*,0)\), since there are no real denominator zeros there.

First exclude \(c_*\le0\). On \(G\le0\) the two real branch points are nonpositive. The integrand of \(zJ(z)\) on this set is bounded by one and tends to zero away from \(G=0\), since for a nonzero \(G\) the two branch points are distinct. On \(G>0\), the semialgebraic Łojasiewicz inequality gives \(G\ge C A^N\) on \(0<A<\epsilon\) for some \(C,N>0\), because \(A\le0\) wherever \(G=0\). In fact if \(A=s\) attains values with \(s>0\), then on those levels \(G>0\): \(G\le0\) would imply \(A\le c_*\). The positive minimum of \(G\) on \(A=s>0\) is semialgebraic and has a power lower bound as \(s\downarrow0\).

For nonconstant \(A\), the [Łojasiewicz gradient inequality](https://cs.nyu.edu/~exact/pap/mesh/Kurdyka_EffectiveGradientIneq.pdf) in sphere charts yields \(\|\nabla_M A\|\ge C' s^{1-\alpha}\) on \(A=s\) for small positive \(s\), with \(\alpha>0\). The constants can be uniform by compactness of the zero fibre. The areas of the levels \(A=s\) are uniformly bounded by [spherical Crofton](https://arxiv.org/abs/math/0610270): on a great circle \(A-s\) is a trigonometric polynomial of bounded degree, so almost every intersection has uniformly bounded cardinality. Coarea gives a pushforward density bound \(O(s^{\alpha-1})\) for small \(s>0\). If \(A\) is constant under \(c_*\le0\), then \(A\le0\) because \(Z\ne\varnothing\), and dominated convergence applies without this density estimate.

On \(G>0\) outside \(A\in[z/2,2z]\), the integrand of \(zJ(z)\) is bounded by two and tends pointwise to zero. In the window the contribution is at most
\[
         O\!\left(z^\alpha\int_{z/2}^{2z}
                     \frac{ds}{\sqrt{(z-s)^2+c_0z^N}}\right)
                      =O\bigl(z^\alpha(1+|\log z|)\bigr).
\]
Thus \(zJ(z)\to0\), contradicting the prescribed polynomial in \(1/z\). Here the zero set of the nonzero regular function \(G\) has area zero.

Now \(c_*>0\). If \(c_*>b_+\), a maximizing point \(x_0\) has \(G<0\). The upper real branch is smooth there, and its deficit from \(c_*\) is \(O(|x-x_0|^2)\) by stationarity. The lower branch stays strictly below \(c_*\). As \(z=c_*+\delta\downarrow c_*\) the contribution to \(-J'(z)\) from this neighborhood is bounded below by a positive multiple of
\[
                         \int_{|x|<\epsilon}
                                      (\delta+|x|^2)^{-3/2}\,dx.
\]
It diverges in dimensions two and three. The integrand of \(-J'\) is nonnegative throughout \(G\le0\). On the closure of \(G>0\) the denominator is uniformly bounded away from zero near \(z=c_*\), because no point of \(Z\) has \(A=c_*\). Hence the other contribution is bounded and cannot cancel. This contradicts the Cauchy identity at \(c_*>0\). It follows that \(c_*=b_+>0\). Apply the argument to \(-P\) for the left endpoint and the branch inequalities.

At an extremal contact, a nonzero \(dG\) would give a line with \(G=-a s+O(s^2)\) on one side, \(a>0\). Along it the square-root branch moves by order \(\sqrt{s}\), contradicting the branch inequality because \(A\) moves by at most order \(s\). Once \(dG=0\), a negative Hessian direction also contradicts that inequality: the two opposite rays both have a positive square-root increment of order \(|s|\), while the first derivatives of \(A\) on them have opposite signs. In particular, at a simultaneous zero of \(B,C\) with independent differentials the quadratic part of \(G\) is indefinite, so such a contact is impossible.

Suppose every rightmost contact on \(Z\) has a local signed square root \(y\) of \(G\) with \(dy\ne0\). Use \(y\) as the normal coordinate and \(x\) tangent to its zero hypersurface. The frozen normal derivative estimate from the regular-zero-level proof applies with \(p=z-b_++b_+-A(x,0)\). It gives \(-c_1(x)/p+O(1+|\log p|)\) with \(c_1(x)>0\). At a maximizing contact the nonnegative deficit \(b_+-A(x,0)\) is \(O(|x|^2)\), so integration in one or two tangential coordinates makes \(J'(z)\) diverge to \(-\infty\). A nonnegative partition of unity preserves the sign of the leading contribution, and the logarithmic errors are bounded by half the leading magnitude plus a constant. Away from these neighborhoods the contribution on \(G>0\) is bounded. Any contribution on \(G<0\) has the same derivative sign since \(z>b_+\). This contradicts the polynomial derivative at \(b_+>0\).

In dimension two, at a rightmost contact with \(dA=0\) and \(G=O(|x|^4)\), the denominator of the positive integrand is at most a constant times \(z-b_++|x|^2\), so \(J(z)\) itself diverges. Apply the last two arguments at the leftmost contacts using \(-P\). The statements about regular and transverse zeros, and about \(G=R^2\), follow from the contact conditions just proved.

The exclusions extend to real closed fields. The eventual moment identities descend to an ordered finitely generated field of coefficients, and the contact conditions are semialgebraic over its real closure. For the condition of a simple signed square root at a zero contact, it suffices that the tangent Hessian have rank one and that the local zero set be a hypersurface. In an analytic local chart, solve for the unique normal critical graph using the nonzero normal Hessian. The local expansion is a unit times the square of displacement from that graph plus its restriction to the graph. A hypersurface zero set makes that restriction identically zero. In the case \(G=R^2\), the relevant tests are directly polynomial in \(A,R\) and their derivatives.


**Corollary (Every open real branch interval needs positive radius on its center levels).**

Under the same eventual-vanishing hypothesis, fix any point \(x\) with \(G(x)<0\). Every nonzero \(s\) strictly between \(A(x)-\sqrt{-G(x)}\) and \(A(x)+\sqrt{-G(x)}\) must occur as \(A(y)\) at some \(y\) with \(G(y)>0\). On compact subintervals avoiding zero and the two endpoints, the largest positive value of \(G\) on each such center level is bounded below by a positive constant. In particular, even when the magnitude of \(BC\) varies within center levels, a candidate cannot have \(BC>0\) if the sign of \(BC\) is constant on each center level and \(A\) is nonconstant.

Use the rotational measure from the center-dependent trinomial argument, without assuming any dependence of \(G\) on \(A\). Where \(G\ge0\), send a sample to the circle of height \(A\) and radius \(\sqrt G\). Where \(G<0\), send it to the point on the axis with coordinate \(A+\sqrt{-G}\cos\theta\), with \(\theta\) uniform. The rotationally invariant solid harmonic moments are precisely the integrated scalar constant terms. Hence eventual vanishing makes the Newtonian potential on the unbounded complementary component a finite multipole expansion about zero. The equality first holds outside a large ball by the harmonic expansion of the kernel, then everywhere on that component by harmonic continuation away from zero.

Near the chosen sample \(x\), an open set of source samples of positive measure has axial arcsine laws with a common interval containing \(s\). Their densities have a uniform positive lower bound on a smaller interval. Thus the potential at cylindrical radius \(\delta\) and height \(s\) diverges at least as a positive constant times \(\log(1/\delta)\). If no sample has \(A=s,G>0\), the whole outward radial ray at height \(s\) and radius \(\delta>0\) lies outside the support, so the potential equals the finite multipole expression there. That expression is bounded as \(\delta\downarrow0\) because \(s\ne0\). This contradiction proves existence. The same estimates are uniform on a compact subinterval as in the statement: use a common neighborhood of \(x\) and a positive margin at its two branch endpoints. The ray is exterior for every radius exceeding \(\sup_{A=s}\sqrt{G_+}\). If those suprema approach zero, the logarithmic lower bound contradicts uniform boundedness of the finite multipole expression on the exterior rays near the axis.

For the sign assertion, a point with \(G<0\) lies in an open set with that sign. A nonconstant real polynomial on the sphere cannot be constant on an open set, so one can choose such a point with \(A\ne0\). Apply existence at its own center level. The existence assertion and this sign obstruction transfer to real closed fields by ordered specialization.

On the ordinary unit two-sphere, the control \(A=x_3,\ B=1,\ C=-(1-x_3^2)/4\) has conditional moments \(\mathcal L_m(x_3)\) and is pure. At the two contact points the Hessian of \(G=1-x_3^2\) is positive definite on the tangent plane, so neither contact admits a simple signed square root. This gives an admissible local contact pattern. The obstruction for exceptional phases needs the invariant structure in addition to the contact tests.


## Connected stabilizers from repeated classical modules

**Theorem (Regular witnesses with connected compact stabilizer and commutant).**

Use real closed \(K\) and the classical sign torsor \(X\) of power-of-two negative rank \(r\), with the block theorem's lower bounds on \(r\). In the orthogonal and special unitary cases let \(n\ge r(r+1)\), and let \(H\) be the connected standard special isometry group of rank \(r\) acting on \(r\) identical positive blocks of rank \(r\). In the quaternionic case let \(n\ge r(2r+1)\), and let \(H=\mathrm{Sp}(r)\) act on \(2r\) identical positive blocks of quaternionic rank \(r\). In all three cases \(H\) acts diagonally on these blocks and fixes the other lines. Then \(X/H\) is point-free and has a regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample. The diagonal invariant field has level \(er\). At the indicated minimum values of \(n\), the commuting identity component is compact.

The representation argument for finite stabilizers applies to a reductive stabilizer of positive dimension whenever the signed block decomposition is available and \(S\times H\) has trivial geometric generic stabilizer on \(X\). The generic invariant fibre theorem makes the orbit over the generic quotient geometrically transitive under \(S\times H\), and freeness makes it a torsor. Project its cocycle to the source and target groups and compare the twisted forms. The same level lower bound follows from a smooth point of \(X/H\) over the field of level \(er\), and the resulting split rank-one action on the generic orbit is active by freeness.

Choose \(S\) with the smallest allowed block size \(d\). We check freeness over the geometric field. First suppose \(n\) is minimal. In the defining geometric module, let \(V_0\) be the fixed negative block and \(U\) the sum of the repeated positive blocks. In the orthogonal and special unitary cases \(\dim V_0=r\) and \(U\) is the space of \(r\)-by-\(r\) matrices, with \(H\) acting by left multiplication by its standard representation. Pull back the first target block to a \(d=r+1\) plane \(W\). A generic such plane projects onto \(V_0\) with \(W\cap U\) a line spanned by an invertible matrix, and does not contain \(V_0\). These open conditions hold for a plane formed from one such line and a nontrivial graph over \(V_0\). In the orthogonal case choose the line spanned by the identity matrix, which is nonisotropic, and choose the graph into its perpendicular with sufficiently small nonzero coefficients so that \(W\) is nondegenerate. These planes occur as pullbacks by geometric isometries. An element of \(H\) preserving \(W\) preserves its invertible-matrix line, so is scalar. A nonidentity scalar that preserves \(W\) would force \(W\) to contain \(V_0\) by projection onto its fixed eigenspace. This proves triviality.

In the quaternionic case the defining complex symplectic module has \(\dim V_0=2r\) and \(U\) is the space of \(2r\)-by-\(2r\) matrices, again with left multiplication by the standard symplectic group. Here pull back the standard complex plane in a single target block, an isotropic \(d=2r+1\) plane \(W\). A generic \(W\) projects onto \(V_0\) with \(W\cap U\) an invertible-matrix line. Choose such a line \(\ell\). In
\[
                         \ell^\perp/\ell
                     = (U\cap\ell^\perp)/\ell\perp V_0
\]
take the graph of an anti-symplectic isomorphism from \(V_0\) to a symplectic \(2r\)-plane in the first summand, which has dimension \(4r^2-2\ge2r\). Its inverse image gives the desired isotropic \(W\). As before a stabilizing element is scalar, and its nonidentity possibility would make \(V_0\subset W\), contrary to isotropy. For \(n\) larger than the minimum, there is an additional fixed subspace. Use the same plane constructed inside the minimal summand to witness the open conditions: \(W\) has a one-dimensional intersection with the complement of \(V_0\), whose projection to \(U\) is an invertible-matrix line, and \(W\) is not invariant under the projection to the full fixed space of \(H\). These conditions again force a stabilizing element to be the identity. Since \(S\) preserves the chosen target plane, the generic intersection of \(S\) with the transported \(H\) is trivial.

In the orthogonal and special unitary cases the twist of the positive template is by its connected special isometry group, so its determinant class is trivial in the appropriate square or norm quotient. The template multiplicity is \(r\). This is the determinant case of the Witt comparison. In the quaternionic case the multiplicity is \(2r\), so the determinant-free comparison applies. Thus the exact level is \(er\), and the standard form of \(S\) has an active split rank-one action on the generic fibre. The regular witness follows.

Every source twist by an \(H\)-cocycle preserves a fixed negative vector, so \(X/H\) has no \(K\)-point. At minimal \(n\), the fixed subspace of \(H\) is negative definite and the positive subspace has no fixed vector. The centralizer preserves these two definite spaces and is compact. The connected normalizer acts on \(H\) by inner automorphisms, or trivially if \(H=\mathrm{SO}(2)\), so its quotient by \(H\) has compact identity component. In particular the quaternionic case with \(r=1\) already yields a regular witness in rank three with connected stabilizer \(\mathrm{Sp}(1)\).


## A restriction on descending the complex exceptional witnesses

**Theorem (The scalar weight substitution does not make a complex witness real and pure).**

Let \(X\) be geometrically integral with a compact probability trace that is invariant under a left circle and strictly positive on nonzero absolute squares. Suppose \(U\) is a complex regular function with finitely many left weights, write \(U=\sum_j U_j\), and assume \(\Lambda(U^m)=0\) for all \(m\ge1\). Put \(V(t)=\sum_j t^j U_j\) on the product with a split scalar, and let conjugation fix \(t\). Then the following hold.

- If \(s\) is the least positive integer for which the zero left weight \(H_s=(U^s)_0\) is nonzero, each real phase \(\alpha V+\overline{\alpha V}\), with \(\alpha\ne0\), has the strictly positive \(2s\)-th trace
\[
                    \binom{2s}{s}|\alpha|^{2s}\Lambda(|H_s|^2).
\]
The real phase \(V\overline V\) has a strictly positive \(s\)-th trace.
- If all the \(H_s\) vanish, \(V\) has only strictly positive or only strictly negative scalar exponents, unless \(U=0\). Its real linear combinations with its conjugate and its norm \(V\overline V\) are safe.

The same statements hold with \(t\) replaced by any nonzero integer power of \(t\). In particular, for each of the published complex exceptional witnesses, this procedure fails at \(s=1\), even though its original complex pure moments all vanish.

After expansion of \(V^j\overline V^{\,k}\), the scalar and left weight conditions are \(u+v=0\) and \(u-v=0\), with \(u\) the sum of original weights in \(V^j\) and \(v\) the sum of original weights in the conjugated copy before conjugation. Thus the integrated constant term is \(\Lambda(H_j\overline H_k)\). In the linear combination at order \(2s\), all mixed terms except \(j=k=s\) have a positive index below \(s\). The two pure terms vanish by the hypothesis on \(U\). For the norm the same calculation at order \(s\) gives the absolute square. If all \(H_s\) vanish, evaluate the Laurent polynomial \(V\) at any compact point where all its nonzero coefficient functions are nonzero. Such points exist by regularity, geometric integrality and Zariski density of the compact quotient. All positive constant terms vanish at the chosen point, so the Laurent power theorem forces the scalar support to lie strictly on one side of zero. That support makes all marked moments eventually zero for any fixed regular Laurent multiplier.

For the exceptional witnesses of [van Rijn, Theorem 1.2](https://hexagonmath.org/2611.00556v1), let \(n\) be the degree of the binary form \(f\), use \(b=n/2,\ a=b-1\), and write \(F=f\) on the bottom row, \(U_0=f\) on the top row, and \(D\) for the lowering derivation. The weight-zero component of the published phase is
\[
       U_0^a D^{(n-1)b}(U_0^b)/((n-1)b)! .
\]
This is nonzero as a homogeneous polynomial, as seen by evaluating at two equal rows: the derivative coefficient becomes a nonzero binomial multiple of \(f^{a+b}\). A nonzero homogeneous polynomial cannot vanish on all matrices of determinant one, since dilation from these matrices is dominant on the full matrix space. Thus \(H_1\ne0\). The statements over real closed fields follow by ordered specialization of the polynomial trace identities and square positivity.

**Proposition (A strict row-shear margin forces safety).**

For either published invariant binary form \(f\) of degree \(n\), let \(R=f(\mathrm{row}_2+F\mathrm{row}_1)/F\), with \(F=f(\mathrm{row}_2)\), and consider \((f(\mathrm{row}_1))^a R^b\) for positive integers \(a,b\). Put \(c=na/(n-2)\) and \(J_0=n/2\). If \(b<c<nb/(n-1)\), the phase is pure and safe. At \(c=nb/(n-1)\) it has strictly positive moments at all orders for which \(cm\) is integral. If \(0<c<b\) and \(nb/((n-1)c)\) is either an integer or has fractional part greater than \(1/(n-1)\), it has nonzero pure moments of arbitrarily large order. The latter condition includes the whole band \(c<b<2(n-1)c/n\). These results show why inserting a fixed margin to remove inverse powers in the complex calculation does not preserve the published counterexample mechanism.

For a weight component of a fixed multiplier of left weight \(w\), expand as in the published proof. At order \(m\) the only possible term has index \(k=cm+w/(n-2)\). If it occurs, it is the constant multiple of
\[
               U_0^{am} F^{\,k-bm}D^{nbm-k}(U_0^{bm}).
\]
When \(b<c<nb/(n-1)\), the exponent \(k-bm\) is eventually nonnegative. Since \(DF=0\), integration by parts kills the term as soon as \(nbm-k>nam+\deg_{\mathrm{top}}\) of the multiplier. Both inequalities hold eventually in each of its finitely many weights, and hold for every contributing pure moment. At the upper equality the derivative order for the unmarked moment is exactly \(nam\). Taking all derivatives on the top factor produces \(F^{am}\), times a positive factorial, and the remaining integrand is \(F^{bm}U_0^{bm}\). Here \(F=\overline U_0\) on the compact form for both binary forms. The sign from integration by parts is positive since \(n\) is even. This gives the positive moment.

For the lower band choose large primes \(\ell=(n-1)cm+1\) with \(cm\) integral and \(\ell\equiv1\bmod J_0\). These occur in a fixed Dirichlet progression after requiring \(m\) to be a multiple of the denominator of \(c\) times \(J_0\). The balanced polynomial at \(k=cm\) has degree \(\ell-1\) in each row; polynomiality also when \(k<bm\) follows by counting the factors contributing a positive shear exponent. The equal-row congruence and binary coefficient in [the published proof, equations (3.1) and (4.6)](https://hexagonmath.org/pdf/2611.00556v1) give
\[
             \ell\,\Lambda(P^m)
                \equiv \binom{nbm}{cm}
                    [X^{\ell-1}Y^{\ell-1}]f(X,Y)^{(\ell-1)/J_0}
                              \pmod\ell .
\]
The binary coefficient is nonzero at the indicated good primes. Write \(nb/((n-1)c)=q+\theta\) with \(0\le\theta<1\). For large primes the least digit of \(nbm\) in base \(\ell\) is \(\ell-q\) if \(\theta=0\), and \(\theta(\ell-1)-q\) otherwise. Under the stated condition it is at least \(cm=(\ell-1)/(n-1)\), so the binomial is a unit. This proves the nonvanishing claim.


## Odd quaternionic sign ranks with complex-unitary stabilizer

**Theorem (Two complementary planes give regular witnesses on odd quaternionic twists).**

Let \(K\) have characteristic zero, \(D=(-1,-1)_K\) be division, and \(E=K(i)\subset D\). Let \(h_0,h_1\) be two rank-\(n\) quaternionic Hermitian forms with diagonal coefficients in \(K^\times\), and let \(B_0,B_1\) be the corresponding \(E\)-Hermitian forms. Suppose the ratio of the products of the diagonal coefficients is \(-1\) modulo \(\mathrm{Nrd}(D^\times)\). Let \(H\) be a reductive subgroup of the standard \(\mathrm{SU}(B_0)\) inside the quaternionic source group. Then the quotient of the isometry torsor \(X=\operatorname{Isom}(h_0,h_1)\) by \(H\) has a regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample in either case below.

- The scalar form of a coordinate-line complement \(B'_1\subset B_1\) contains five squares as a subform. Here \(S=\mathrm{SU}(B'_1)\) in the target fixes the omitted line pointwise.
- The scalar form of \(B_1\) contains five squares, \(H\) stabilizes a nondegenerate coordinate line of \(B_0\), and either \(n\) is odd or \(H\) does not contain the geometric scalar \(-I_n\). Here \(S=\mathrm{SU}(B_1)\).

In particular for the sign forms \(h_0=I_{n-r}\perp-I_r\), \(h_1=I_n\), with odd \(r\) and \(n\ge4\), the first case covers every such reductive \(H\), with no assumption that it fixes a source line. At \(n=3\) the second case covers the coordinate-line stabilizers. The invariant field has level \(\min(s(K),4)\) for these sign forms. Over real closed \(K\) each such quotient is point-free. This includes a connected compact stabilizer with compact commuting identity component over a real closed field by taking \(H=\mathrm{SU}(n-r)\) on the positive block when \(n-r\ge2\).

We verify that \(S\times H\) has trivial geometric generic stabilizer on \(X\). After geometric base change the quaternionic space is symplectic, and each embedded special unitary group preserves the two complementary Lagrangian planes from the complex structure. Choose dual coordinates on the source planes so that the action is \(g\) and \(g^{-\mathsf T}\) on them. A generic isometry pulls back the two target planes to the graphs of symmetric matrices \(M,N\) from the first source plane to its dual, with \(M-N\) nonsingular. Preserving both graphs requires \(g\) to preserve both symmetric matrices.

In the first case the generic pair is nonsingular with simple relative spectrum, so a preserving \(g\) has diagonal signs in eigen-coordinates. The vector on the first target plane along the fixed coordinate line pulls back to a vector of the first graph with all its first-source-plane coordinates nonzero in that eigenbasis, generically. A diagonal sign preserving that vector is the identity. These are nonempty open conditions: take \(M\) to be the identity and \(N\) diagonal with distinct entries different from one, and take the vector in the first graph with all first-plane coordinates one. Geometric isometries act transitively on ordered transverse Lagrangian pairs with a specified nonzero vector in the first plane, so this satisfies the required pullback conditions.

In the second case \(g=\operatorname{diag}(g',\lambda)\), using the invariant coordinate line and its orthogonal complement under the source Hermitian form. The generic restricted pair of symmetric matrices on the first \(n-1\) coordinates has simple relative spectrum, so \(g'\) is a diagonal sign matrix. The mixed column of \(M\) has every entry nonzero in the joint eigenbasis, and the last diagonal entry is nonzero. Preserving \(M\) forces \(g'=\lambda^{-1}I\) and \(\lambda^2=1\). This leaves only \(I\) under the stated condition. The generic conditions can be checked with the restricted blocks the identity and a diagonal with distinct entries different from one, common mixed column with every entry one, and nonzero distinct last diagonal entries. These choices give transverse graphs. This proves freeness in both cases.

Write \(F=K(X/H)^S\). The geometrically transitive generic \(S\times H\) orbit is a torsor by the generic invariant fibre theorem and freeness. Its two projections twist \(B_0,B_1\) by determinant-one complex isometries, giving Hermitian forms with the original determinant classes modulo \(N_{E_F/F}(E_F^\times)\), where the split étale convention applies if needed. Their quaternionifications are isometric over \(F\). The ratio of their diagonal determinants is therefore a reduced norm. This follows from the Pfaffian identity for quaternionic Hermitian matrices, as in the central isometry torsor calculation. Since the complex norm subgroup lies in the quaternion reduced norm subgroup, the hypothesis gives \(-1\in\mathrm{Nrd}(D_F^\times)\). The Hamilton norm is the sum of four squares, so \(s(F)\le4\). The standard scalar form of \(S\) is isotropic over \(F\) by its five-square subform, or \(S\) is split if \(E_F\) splits. This yields an active split rank-one action on the generic fibre and the regular witness.

For the diagonal sign forms of odd \(r\), the difference of scalar Witt classes is \(8r\langle1\rangle\) up to sign. The torsor has a point over \(K\) if \(s(K)\le4\), or over \(K(S_4^-)\) otherwise, by Hermitian Witt cancellation. The image is a smooth point of the quotient, giving the exact level lower bound by completion as in the block theorem. Over a real closed field, any twist by an \(H\)-cocycle preserves the norm class of the complex determinant. The source Hermitian form still has odd negative index, so its quaternionification cannot be isometric to the definite target. This proves point-freeness. In the indicated compact connected example the entire fixed quaternionic space is negative definite and the positive block has no fixed vector. The centralizer is compact, and the connected normalizer acts by inner automorphisms, giving the assertion on the commuting identity component.


## Weighted interval densities and angular monodromy

**Theorem (No polynomial weighted density for linear invariant heights).**

On the pointed compact octahedral quotient take \(x\) to be any nonconstant affine-linear combination of \(p=p_2\) and \(q=p_3\) on its angular sphere quotient. On the icosahedral quotient take any nonconstant affine-linear combination of \(p,h\) from (H4). For any real regular function \(D\ne0\) on the full compact quotient, the pushforward of \(D^{2m}\) times Haar probability by \(x\) has no polynomial density for any \(m\ge1\). More strongly, a single nonzero local analytic density on an open interval of regular values cannot equal a single-valued rational function of the height. In particular a weighted interval witness with this coordinate is impossible, irrespective of how the weight vanishes at its critical points. The exclusion also holds after replacing \(x\) by a polynomial in \(x\) that is injective on its compact range.

Begin by applying the left-circle Reynolds projection to \(D^{2m}\), obtaining an angular regular function \(L\). It is nonnegative on the real angular quotient, and its trace is strictly positive. The complex oriented angular plane covers have equations
\[
       J^2=q\,\Delta(p,q),\qquad
       \Delta=p^2-4p^3-4q-27q^2+18pq
       \quad\text{or}\quad J^2=f(p,h),
\]
where in the second equation \(f\) is the polynomial written on the right of (H4) with the sphere norm set to one and with a constant rescaling of \(J\). The area form is a nonzero constant times \(dp\wedge dq/J\) or \(dp\wedge dh/J\), by the invariant Jacobian formula. For a generic height value \(s\), the conditional affine curve \(C_s^\circ\) is a smooth double cover of an affine line in a coordinate \(z\). Its compactification \(C_s\) is elliptic or of genus two. Let \(\omega_s=dz/J\), holomorphic on the compactification. On a physical open interval of regular values the density is, up to a fixed nonzero normalization, the period of \(L\,\omega_s\) over the sum of the real compact ovals that carry area. Orient these ovals to make \(\omega_s\) positive. The same cycles give a positive period for the unweighted area. These assertions follow by coarea on the oriented quotient, avoiding the finitely many height values of its singular points and of singular fibres. The compact real angular level has no boundary on the remaining smooth affine curve, so it is a union of whole ovals.

In particular the cycle \(\gamma_s\) has nonzero image in \(H_1(C_s,\mathbb Q)\), because the holomorphic differential \(\omega_s\) has nonzero period on it. The weighted differential has poles only at infinity, and its period is a functional on \(H_1(C_s^\circ,\mathbb Q)\). If the density agrees locally with a rational function, that period is single-valued under analytic continuation on the smooth parameter line with the rational poles removed. Therefore the period functional vanishes on the span of all \(g\gamma_s-\gamma_s\), where \(g\) ranges over monodromy. Removing finitely many additional parameter values does not change the monodromy image. We show that this span is the whole affine homology for each pencil.

First consider the cubic cases, namely the two individual octahedral invariants and the icosahedral invariant \(p\). For the height \(p\) on the octahedral quotient the cubic \(q\,\Delta\) at \(p=1/4\) is \(q^2(1-54q)/2\), with exactly one double root. For the height \(q\), divide the equation by its nonzero parameter \(q\) after adjoining its square root. At \(q=0\) the resulting cubic \(\Delta(p,0)=p^2(1-4p)\) has the same kind of degeneration. For the icosahedral height \(p\), the cubic in \(h\) at \(p=0\) is \(h^2(4-25h)\). The discriminants are nonzero generically, as one also verifies directly from the displayed polynomials. In each case the three-root ratio is nonconstant, since two roots collide while the third remains distinct. After a finite base change and a squared scaling of the vertical coordinate, the family becomes a pullback of the Legendre family along this nonconstant root ratio. Away from finitely many branch values this map is a finite covering. The [Legendre monodromy](https://www-fourier.ujf-grenoble.fr/~peters/Books/PeriodBook.f/SecondEdition/PerBook.pdf) contains independent transvections around \(0\) and \(1\), so any finite-index subgroup has irreducible rational homology action. The cubic has a single point at infinity, hence its affine and compact first homology agree. For a nonzero cycle the span of its orbit differences is a nonzero invariant subspace, and therefore all of homology.

For the other cases we use the following explicit monodromy criterion. For a degree-\(d\) polynomial double cover of the affine line with distinct branch roots, reduction of affine homology modulo two identifies it with the even subsets of the \(d\) finite roots. An arc between consecutive roots lifts to a cycle corresponding to their pair, and the \(d-1\) consecutive arcs form a basis. This follows by lifting a tree joining the roots, which gives a spine of the branched affine cover. The half-twists on consecutive roots act by transvections on their lifted cycles, and modulo two these are exactly the adjacent permutations on the even-subset space. Thus the homology reduction of any lifted joining arc is the vector of its pair. A fibre with exactly one pair colliding and all others distinct has monodromy a nontrivial power of the transvection along that lifted arc. Indeed near the collision the half-difference of the roots, after any necessary quadratic base change, is a vanishing local parameter to positive order. If the graph of monodromy translates of the colliding pair is connected on the finite roots, their lifted cycles span affine homology over \(\mathbb Q\) by reduction modulo two. Since \(\gamma_s\) has nonzero compact class, it pairs nontrivially with at least one translate. The corresponding transvection power puts that translate into the span of the differences. The span is monodromy invariant, hence contains the full orbit and gives all affine homology. This argument also handles the two points at infinity in the quartic case.

For a mixed octahedral height normalize it to \(s=q+a p\) with \(a\ne0\). The fibre polynomial has degree four in \(p\). One root comes from \(q=0\), and the other three, from \(\Delta(p,s-ap)\), are permuted transitively since the discriminant curve \(\Delta=0\) is irreducible. For instance it is parametrized by the repeated root of the original cubic \(Z^3-Z^2+pZ-q\). To find a collision with the distinguished root, the cubic discriminant and the resultant with the distinguished factor are
\[
 \operatorname{disc}_p\Delta(p,s-ap)
       =16(9a-27s+1)^3(a^3+a^2+s),\qquad
 \operatorname{Res}_p(\Delta(p,s-ap),s-ap)=s^2(a-4s).
\]
At \(s=a/4\) the cubic is squarefree unless \(a=-4/9\) or \(-1/2\), and at either exceptional slope it is squarefree at \(s=0\). In the chosen fibre the distinguished root meets one cubic root and the other two stay distinct. The orbit of that pair joins the distinguished root to each cubic root, so the graph criterion applies.

For a mixed icosahedral height, or for \(h\) alone, normalize it to \(s=h+a p\). The fibre is a quintic in \(p\) with constant nonzero leading coefficient. The discriminant curve \(f=0\) is irreducible, since all reflecting mirrors have one common image in the reflection quotient. Its coordinate ring remains integral after this linear change, so the five finite roots are permuted transitively. For all real slopes there is a fibre with exactly one double root and the others distinct. At \(s=0\) the polynomial is \(p^2\) times a cubic whose constant term and discriminant are
\[
        4(a^2-4a-16),\qquad -6400(a-7)(3a+4)^3(5a-36)^5 .
\]
Thus this fibre works off the five exceptional slopes. At those slopes use the discriminant factorization
\[
 \operatorname{disc}_p f(p,s-ap)
     =-131072000\,s^2(3a-81s+4)^3(5a+25s-36)^5\,Q(a,s),
\]
where
\[
 \begin{split}
 Q={}&a^5-15a^4+40a^3+240a^2-640a-1792\\
    &+(1000a^2-12000a+35200)s+10000s^2 .
 \end{split}
\]
The discriminant of \(Q\) in \(s\) is \(-40000(a-8)^5\), so its two roots are distinct at all five slopes. Its values at \((3a+4)/81\) and \((36-5a)/25\) are \((9a-8)^3(9a^2-111a+376)/6561\) and \((a-8)^4(a+17)\), respectively, nonzero at the five slopes. At least one root of \(Q\) is nonzero, giving a simple discriminant zero and hence the claimed fibre. The orbit graph of its pair is connected: it has an edge and a transitive action on five vertices, so all its components have equal size dividing five.

In all cases the vanishing of the period on orbit differences therefore forces its value on the physical cycle to be zero. The weight \(L\) has positive total mass, so the density is positive on some regular interval. This contradicts a polynomial density. The same argument applies after an injective polynomial reparametrization of the compact height range: a polynomial density in the new variable pulls back to a polynomial density in the old one, using the constant sign of the derivative on the interior. The angular and scalar moment formulas, and the moments required by a proposed polynomial-density identity of fixed degrees, descend to finitely generated ordered coefficient fields. Equality of pushforward measures on the compact interval is determined by polynomial moments. Thus ordered specialization gives the exclusion over real closed fields as well.


## Galois fields of scalar Laurent Cauchy transforms

**Theorem (A normal scalar Cauchy field is rational).**

Let \(k\) be algebraically closed of characteristic zero, and let \(P(t)\in k(t)\). Take formal scalar constant term at \(t=0\). If the field generated over \(k(z)\) by
\[
             \mathcal C_P(z)=\operatorname{CT}_t\frac1{z-P(t)}
\]
is normal, it is a rational function field over \(k\). For nonconstant \(P\), its degree over \(k(z)\) is at most the number of poles of \(P\) on the projective line. In particular, if \(P\) is Laurent with poles at both zero and infinity, a normal Cauchy field is quadratic and \(P=A t^d+B+C t^{-d}\) with \(d\ge1\) and \(AC\ne0\). Conversely every such trinomial has a quadratic Cauchy field.

If \(P\) is regular at zero the transform is \(1/(z-P(0))\). Otherwise let \(a>0\) be its pole order there. In a splitting field of \(P(t)=z\), let \(\mathcal I\) be the \(a\) branches tending to zero over \(z=\infty\). The local residue formula for Laurent moments, as in [Duistermaat and van der Kallen, §2](https://webspace.science.uu.nl/~kalle101/powers.pdf), gives
\[
                  \mathcal C_P(z)=-\sum_{i\in\mathcal I}\frac{t_i'(z)}{t_i(z)}.
\]
Indeed the meromorphic integrand is \(dt/[t(z-P(t))]\). Near zero it has no residue at zero and each of the small roots contributes the displayed residue. The calculation can be made over the complex numbers after choosing a finitely generated field of definition, or in Puiseux expansions at infinity.

Let \(G\) be the geometric monodromy group. The Cauchy germ has stabilizer exactly the set stabilizer of \(\mathcal I\). In one direction this follows from the sum formula. For the other, at infinity a small branch has \(t_i'/t_i\sim-1/(az)\). A branch tending to a finite nonzero pole has logarithmic derivative \(o(z^{-1})\), while a branch tending to infinity has leading logarithmic derivative \(1/(bz)\), where \(b\) is the pole order there. If a translate contains fewer than \(a\) small branches, its sum with the negative sign has coefficient strictly less than one at \(z^{-1}\). Thus equality with the original germ forces preservation of \(\mathcal I\).

If the field is normal, its stabilizer \(N\) fixes every translate of \(\mathcal I\). Partition the roots by their membership in these translates. Monodromy at infinity belongs to \(N\) and each of its cycles belongs to one part. The part containing a small branch is consequently exactly \(\mathcal I\): the full small cycle lies in it, and \(\mathcal I\) itself distinguishes these branches from the others. By transitivity the parts are the translates of \(\mathcal I\), so there are \([G:N]\) parts and each is a union of pole cycles. This gives the degree bound. The point stabilizer of a small branch lies in \(N\), since it fixes that branch's part. Thus the Cauchy field is a subfield of \(k(t)\), and Lüroth gives its rationality.

In the Laurent case with poles at both ends, the partition has exactly two parts. It follows that \(a=b\) and the field is quadratic. Choose its rational parameter \(u\). The two poles of \(z\) on this intermediate line are simple, since the inertia at infinity acts trivially on the two parts. Choose the zero and pole of \(u\) at those two points. Each point has a single preimage on the \(t\)-line, one at zero and one at infinity. Hence \(u\) is a constant times \(t^{\pm a}\), and \(z\) has the asserted trinomial form. Conversely scalar extraction for such a trinomial yields \(((z-B)^2-4AC)^{-1/2}\), which generates a quadratic field.

**Corollary (Exact averaging of the sphere witness over distinct axes cannot be rational).**

Fix distinct unit axes \(\xi_j\) in the geometric rotation orbit of an axis on the unit sphere and put \(v_j=\langle\xi_j,x\rangle\). Take at least two of them, and nonzero weights \(c_j\) with sum one. The averaged conditional scalar moments of the finitely many phases
\[
                 t+v_j+\frac{v_j^2-1}{4t}
\]
cannot be realized as the local scalar moments of any single rational scalar phase over the angular function field, even allowing arbitrary poles and rational angular coefficients. In particular the finite rotation average of the sphere witness cannot produce a phase on either exceptional product by exact scalar moment descent.

Extend the angular function field to an algebraic closure and regard it as the constant field in \(z\). The required Cauchy transform would be
\[
                     \sum_j c_j (z^2-2v_jz+1)^{-1/2}.
\]
The quadratics have simple roots and pairwise disjoint root sets over that constant field: a common root would have to be zero by subtracting the two polynomials, while the constant coefficient is one. Their square classes are independent by the valuations at those roots. The displayed sum has trivial stabilizer in the multiquadratic Galois group since each sign character occurs with a nonzero coefficient. Write \(d\) for the number of axes. The transform generates the full multiquadratic field of degree \(2^d\). It has genus \(1+2^{d-1}(d-2)\) by Riemann–Hurwitz, since the \(2d\) disjoint branch points each have inertia of order two and infinity is unramified. For \(d\ge2\) this is a normal field of positive genus, which the theorem excludes. This concerns equality of the conditional sequences before the final angular integration.


## The rank-three unitary stabilizer boundary

**Theorem (The full special-unitary double quotient has a rational invariant field).**

Let \(D=(-1,-1)_K\) be division over a characteristic-zero field, and let \(n\ge2\). Take the quaternionic isometry torsor \(X\) from \(I_{n-r}\perp-I_r\) to \(I_n\). Let both the target subgroup \(S\) and the source subgroup \(H\) be the full special unitary groups of the standard complex planes in the two forms. Then \(K(X/H)^S\) is a purely transcendental field of degree \(n+2\) over \(K\).

Over \(E=K(i)\), identify the torsor with the split symplectic group by using a reference isometry that multiplies each negative coordinate by the central scalar \(i\) of the coefficient extension. Both special unitary groups become the standard special linear subgroups on complementary Lagrangians. Write the resulting symplectic matrix in blocks as
\[
 g=\begin{pmatrix}P&Q\\R&T\end{pmatrix}.
\]
On the open set with \(P,Q\) invertible, put \(B=P^{-1}Q,\ C=RP^{-1}\); these matrices are symmetric and \(T=CPB+P^{-\mathsf T}\). For the two full general linear groups on complementary Lagrangians, \(P\) transforms as \(sPh^{-1}\), \(B\) as \(hBh^{\mathsf T}\), and \(P^{\mathsf T}CP\) as \(h^{-\mathsf T}P^{\mathsf T}CP h^{-1}\). Thus the characteristic coefficients of \(P^{\mathsf T}T=I+P^{\mathsf T}CPB\) are invariants. Denote the elementary symmetric functions in its eigenvalues \(k_j\) by \(e_j\), and put \(e_0=1\).

Over an algebraic closure, at a generic point one can normalize \(P\) and \(B\) to the identity and diagonalize the remaining symmetric matrix by an orthogonal change of basis. Hence the general linear double orbits are distinguished generically by the unordered \(k_j\). For the special linear double orbits add \(d_P=\det P\) and \(d_Q=\det Q\). If these determinants agree for two points in a generic general linear orbit, the translating pair has determinants either both \(+1\) or both \(-1\), by the respective transformation rules \(\det s/\det h\) and \(\det s\det h\). The latter case can be adjusted by the simultaneous sign flip of one eigenline in the generic general linear stabilizer. Thus the augmented invariants give a single geometric generic orbit. They are algebraically independent: consecutive symplectic coordinate pairs give diagonal \(P,Q,R,T\) with \(P_{jj}T_{jj}=k_j\), \(Q_{jj}R_{jj}=k_j-1\), and the nonzero entries of \(P,Q\) can be chosen to prescribe their determinants independently. The geometric quotient field over \(E\) is therefore \(E(e_1,\ldots,e_n,d_P,d_Q)\).

Let \(\delta=(-1)^r\). The compact conjugation of the symplectic group interchanges \(P,T\) and \(Q,-R\) on entries, together with coefficient conjugation. The reference isometry changes this by the right sign matrix of determinant \(\delta\) on each \(n\)-block. Thus the semilinear descent fixes the \(e_j\) and sends \(d_P\) to \(\delta\det T\), and \(d_Q\) to \(\delta(-1)^n\det R\). The block identities give
\[
          d_P\det T=e_n,\qquad
          d_Q\det R=\prod_j(k_j-1).
\]
Consequently the field has a real model with \(e_1,\ldots,e_n\) and two complex coordinates of norms \(\delta e_n\) and \(\delta\sum_{j=0}^n(-1)^j e_j\). Taking real and imaginary parts of the two determinant coordinates gives four fixed generators. The two norm equations solve for \(e_n\) and \(e_{n-1}\), leaving the four generators and \(e_1,\ldots,e_{n-2}\) algebraically independent. This proves the rationality statement.

**Corollary (The rank-three compact-subgroup test has one complex-unitary boundary).**

In quaternionic rank three with odd negative rank, over a real closed field the quotients by all geometrically proper reductive subgroups of the source special unitary group have regular counterexamples. For the quotient by the full source special unitary group, no connected reductive subgroup of the compact target has active split rank one on its generic invariant fibre. This last assertion determines the reach of the subgroup method, without deciding the trace on the full-unitary quotient.

For the positive assertion take \(S=\mathrm{SU}(3)\) and use the two-plane test. In the geometric fibre, a generic pair of symmetric matrices has simple relative spectrum. Its simultaneous stabilizer in \(\mathrm{SL}_3\) is diagonal signs in a generic eigenbasis. Every nonidentity such sign is noncentral. The conjugacy class of any one of these signs cannot lie in a proper closed subgroup, since its normal closure in \(\mathrm{SL}_3\) is the whole group. Hence for a generic eigenbasis no nonidentity sign belongs to the given proper \(H\), by avoidance of finitely many proper closed sets. This gives generic freeness. The determinant comparison then gives level four and the active split action. The same argument replaces the coordinate-line freeness hypothesis of the full-target part of the two-plane theorem in any rank when \(H\) is geometrically proper and does not contain \(-I_n\) if \(n\) is even.

For the boundary case, the preceding rationality theorem makes the invariant field for \(S=\mathrm{SU}(3)\) formally real. For \(S=\mathrm{Sp}(2)\) fixing a target line, first twist the source by a cocycle in \(H\) to make its negative rank one if it had been three. A sign change on two negative lines is determinant one and gives that twist. The twisted isometry torsor maps equivariantly and dominantly to \(X/H\). Its quotient by \(S\) parametrizes the isometric embeddings of a fixed positive target line in the source, and is smooth with a real point, since the new source has positive lines. Thus its formally real field contains \(K(X/H)^S\). These two subgroup types exhaust the proper simple factors of rank at least two in compact \(\mathrm{Sp}(3)\), by the defining symplectic representation calculation in the small-twist boundary. A larger subgroup containing either type has a subfield of the corresponding formally real field. The function field of \(X/H\) has level at least four by the smooth point over \(K(S_4^-)\), so rank-one compact factors stay anisotropic over all its invariant subfields. The full target has invariant field \(K\). This exhausts the connected reductive subgroups.


## Direct moments on the remaining rank-three special-unitary quotient

**Proposition (The elementary spectral and determinant coordinates give no affine pure phase).**

Use the rank-three full-special-unitary quotient of the previous theorem over a real closed field, with \(r\) odd. The functions \(e_1,d_P,d_Q\) and their conjugates from the double quotient are regular, by their polynomial expressions in the symplectic matrix blocks. In the real affine-linear span of \(e_1\) and the two real components of each determinant, the only pure function is zero. In this span the first four moments already suffice.

Work on the compact geometric reference group \(\mathrm{Sp}(3)\), which computes the normalized trace on the twist by base change. There \(k_1,k_2,k_3\) are the squared cosines of the compact \(U(3)\)-double coset. Their unordered law is the constant-weight Jacobi law of symmetry parameter one, by the [type CI integration formula](https://arxiv.org/abs/math-ph/0111005), with eigenangle change \(k_j=(1+\cos(2\theta_j))/2\). Equivalently on \(0<x<y<z<1\) the ordered density is \(180(y-x)(z-x)(z-y)\). The moduli of the determinants are \(\sqrt N,\sqrt M\), where \(N=xyz\) and \(M=(1-x)(1-y)(1-z)\). Their phases can be independently averaged over two ordinary circles. Indeed left and right \(U(3)\)-translations change the phases by the product and ratio of the two determinants of the translating matrices, and that map of two circles is surjective. These translations fix the spectral variables.

Because \(r\) is odd, the two real components of each regular determinant phase are purely imaginary on the compact reference: the twist sends \(d_P\) to \(-\overline{d_P}\) there, and likewise for \(d_Q\). Normalize the real components as \(d+\sigma d\) and \((d-\sigma d)/i\), where \(\sigma\) is the semilinear descent of functions. Write \(u,v\ge0\) for the sums of the squares of their two real coefficients in each determinant arm. If the coefficient of \(e_1\) is zero, the first moment removes the constant and strict square negativity on the compact reference removes any nonzero imaginary linear combination. Otherwise scale that coefficient to one, and use the first moment to get the center \(A=x+y+z-3/2\).

Write \(\mathbb E\) for the normalized spectral integral. The second and third moments of the resulting phase are
\[
 \mathbb E(A^2)-2(u+v)\mathbb E(N),\qquad -6(u-v)\mathbb E(AN).
\]
Here symmetry interchanges \(N,M\) and negates \(A\). Direct simplex integration gives \(\mathbb E(A^2)=3/28,\ \mathbb E(N)=1/14,\ \mathbb E(AN)=3/140\), so vanishing forces \(u=v=3/8\). The fourth moment is then
\[
 \mathbb E(A^4)-12(u+v)\mathbb E(A^2N)
           +6(u^2+v^2)\mathbb E(N^2)+24uv\mathbb E(NM)=-25/448.
\]
The four entries used here are \(19/560,\,11/840,\,1/84,\,1/420\), respectively. They follow by multiplying the polynomial by the displayed Vandermonde density and using the ordered-simplex monomial integral \(1/[(i+1)(i+j+2)(i+j+k+3)]\) for \(x^i y^j z^k\). The moment identities are rational and pass to every real closed ground field.
