This calculation leaves higher regular spectral and determinant combinations as candidates on the full-unitary rank-three quotient.


## A flat spectral model on the rank-three special-unitary quotient

**Theorem (The spectral and determinant subring has a uniform volume trace).**

Use the rank-three full special-unitary quotient with odd negative rank over a real closed field. Let \(s=e_1\) be the spectral sum. Take real regular determinant components \(u_1,u_2,v_1,v_2\), normalized so that on the compact reference group they are \(i\) times the ordinary real and imaginary parts of \(d_P,d_Q\), respectively. Over \(\mathbb R\), the trace on the polynomial subring generated by these five functions is
\[
   \Lambda(F)=\frac{180}{\pi^2}
      \int_{\mathcal B} F(s,i z_1,i z_2,i w_1,i w_2)\,ds\,dz_1\,dz_2\,dw_1\,dw_2,
\]
where, with \(N=z_1^2+z_2^2\) and \(M=w_1^2+w_2^2\), the real region \(\mathcal B\) is defined by requiring all three roots of
\[
                  T^3-sT^2+(s+N+M-1)T-N
\]
to belong to \([0,1]\). In particular the real height \(s\) has the piecewise polynomial density
\[
 \rho(s)=
 \begin{cases}
  3s^5/8, &0<s<1,\\
  3(15s^4-90s^3+190s^2-165s+51)/8, &1<s<2,\\
  3(3-s)^5/8, &2<s<3.
 \end{cases}
\]
This gives a regular pair with a nonzero marked moment at every positive order on the product of this quotient with a split scalar. The formula for the polynomial subring also gives a direct moment model for the remaining quotient without a scalar.

For the trace formula, pass from the three ordered eigenvalues to their elementary symmetric functions. The absolute Jacobian is their Vandermonde, which cancels the Vandermonde factor in the type CI density of the preceding proposition. Replace \(e_2,e_3\) by \(N=e_3,\ M=1-s+e_2-e_3\). This has unit absolute Jacobian. Conditional on \(s,N,M\), the two determinant phases are independent and uniform, by the bi-unitary translations. The changes \(dN\,d\theta=2\,dz_1\,dz_2\) and \(dM\,d\psi=2\,dw_1\,dw_2\) give the claimed normalization. The real descent indicated in the double quotient theorem explains the imaginary coefficient in each reference determinant coordinate. The five generators are algebraically independent by the invariant field calculation.

For the marginal, put \(a=x,\ b=y-x,\ c=z-y\) in ordered eigenvalue coordinates \(0<x<y<z<1\). Then \(s=3a+2b+c\) and the density in \((a,b,c)\) on the unit simplex is \(180(b^2c+bc^2)\). For a threshold \(s_0<1\), integrate directly over \(3a+2b+c\le s_0\) to get \(s_0^6/16\). For \(1<s_0<2\), subtract the part with \(a+b+c>1\). Put \(c=1-a-b+d\) there, so the removed simplex is \(2a+b+d\le s_0-1\); its integral is
\[
                   (s_0-1)^4((s_0-1)^2-12(s_0-1)+60)/16 .
\]
Differentiation and the symmetry \(s\mapsto3-s\) give the asserted density.

For the regular pair with a split scalar \(t\), put \(x=s/3\), \(T=1+t\),
\[
 \begin{split}
 f(x)&=(1-x)(1-3x)^2(1-3x/2)^2,\\
 p_0&=Tt^{-2}f(Tx)^2,\qquad
 P=p_0^6(1+t^6p_0),\qquad Q=t^{-6}.
 \end{split}
\]
Apply the distinguished-endpoint criterion of the [piecewise-polynomial height construction](PIECEWISE_DENSITY_AND_COMPACT_COADJOINT_ALL_ORDER_COUNTEREXAMPLES.md). The nonzero inner knots are \(1/3\) and \(2/3\), both double zeros of \(f\), and its zero at one is simple with \(f'(1)=-1\). The density of \(x\) has leading inward term \((2187/8)(1-x)^5\) at one, and its pieces have degree at most five. Consequently
\[
          \Lambda(P^m)=0,\qquad
          \Lambda(QP^m)=32805\,\frac{(12m)!}{(12m+6)!}\qquad(m\ge1).
\]
The resulting trace identities are rational and carry over to every real closed field in the stated sign model.


**Proposition (A spectral endpoint obstructs purity for odd determinant phases).**

On the rank-three full special-unitary quotient without a scalar, consider a real regular function
\[
                  P=A(s)+B(s,u_1,u_2,v_1,v_2),
\]
where \(A\) is polynomial and \(B\) is polynomial and odd under simultaneous sign change of all four determinant components. Suppose \(A\) has a unique global extremum of one type on \([0,3]\) at one endpoint, with nonzero derivative there. Negate \(P\) if needed to make it a maximum. At that endpoint one of the determinant circles collapses and the other remains. On the compact reference write \(P=A+iV\). If the first inward spectral derivative of \(V\) is constant on the set of maximizing angles of the restriction of \(V\) to the surviving circle, then \(P\) has infinitely many nonzero pure moments. This condition holds when the maximizing angle is unique, and also when \(V\) and its inward derivative both vanish on the circle. For example, a nonzero linear combination of the surviving determinant components at that endpoint has a unique maximizing angle.

To prove this, use \(x=s\) at zero or \(x=3-s\) at three, and write \(a=A\) at the endpoint. The spectral density in \(x\) is \(3x^5/8\) for \(0<x<1\). Conditional on \(x\) and the ordered eigenvalues, both angles are uniform. The squared radius of the collapsed circle is \(O(x^3)\) and that of the surviving circle is \(1-x+O(x^2)\), uniformly in the remaining spectral variables. Since \(B\) is polynomial, this gives a uniform expansion
\[
     V=V_0(\psi)+xV_1(\psi)+O(x^{3/2}),\qquad
     A=a-\kappa x+O(x^2),\qquad \kappa>0,
\]
with \(\psi\) the surviving angle. The two first coefficients depend only on \(\psi\). In particular the stated inward derivative is well defined.

Let \(I(z)=\Lambda((z-P)^{-1})\) mean the Cauchy integral on the compact reference cycle. Eventual vanishing of the pure moments would make \(I\) a polynomial in \(1/z\) with coefficient one at \(1/z\), throughout \(\operatorname{Re}z>a\) away from zero. Put \(\eta=\max V_0\) and \(z_0=a+i\eta\). For \(a<0\) the integral is analytic at zero. If \(z_0=0\), the integral along positive real \(z=\delta\) stays bounded as \(\delta\downarrow0\), by the \(x^5\) density and \(\operatorname{Re}(\delta-P)\ge\delta+c x\) near the endpoint. Both cases contradict the pole. Assume \(z_0\ne0\).

Set \(z=z_0+\delta e^{i\phi}\) with fixed \(\phi<\pi/2\) close to \(\pi/2\). For \(D=\delta e^{i\phi}+i(\eta-V_0(\psi))\) and \(c(\psi)=\kappa-iV_1(\psi)\), use
\[
                z-P=D+c(\psi)x+O(x^{3/2}).
\]
The real-part bound and triangle inequality give a uniform lower bound by a positive multiple of \(|D|+x\) for the absolute values of both denominators, with or without the remainder. In \(I^{(6)}(z)/6!\), the replacement with the linear denominator causes an error \(O(1+|D|^{-1/2})\) after integrating in the radial variable and averaging the remaining spectral variables and the collapsed angle. Indeed the error is bounded by a constant times \(\int_0^\epsilon x^{13/2}(|D|+x)^{-8}\,dx\). The contribution away from the spectral endpoint is uniformly bounded by uniqueness of the global extremum. Extending the leading radial integral to infinity gives
\[
             \frac38\int_0^\infty
                      \frac{x^5\,dx}{(D+c(\psi)x)^7}
                =\frac{1}{16c(\psi)^6D}.
\]
This follows first for real positive \(D,c\) by the beta integral and then for positive real parts by holomorphic continuation.

On the maximizing set of \(V_0\), the direction of \(c^6\) is constant by the derivative condition. Outside a neighborhood of this set the contribution is bounded. In the neighborhood rotate the leading term by \(i c(\psi_0)^6/|c(\psi_0)|^6\), with any maximizing angle \(\psi_0\). Take the neighborhood small enough and \(\phi\) close enough to \(\pi/2\) to make its rotated real part at least a positive multiple of \(1/|D|\). The error is absorbed by \(x^{-1/2}\le\epsilon x^{-1}+C_\epsilon\). Smoothness gives \(\eta-V_0(\psi)=O((\psi-\psi_0)^2)\) at any maximizing angle, so the integral of \(1/|D|\) diverges. The Cauchy polynomial has bounded sixth derivative at \(z_0\), giving the contradiction. For transfer to arbitrary real closed fields, fix the degrees and an asserted start of eventual vanishing. The moment identities generate a finitely generated polynomial ideal in the phase coefficients, so they are equivalent to a finite subcollection. The endpoint and angle conditions are semialgebraic. A counterexample would therefore give a counterexample over \(\mathbb R\) by real closed field transfer.


## Reflection-odd profiles at stationary fibres

**Theorem (Affine angular centers exclude reflection-odd Laurent profiles).**

Let \(X\) be either full exceptional compact quotient, and let \(A\) be a real affine combination of the two basic angular reflection invariants. Consider
\[
                  P(x,t)=A(x)+\sum_{j=1}^d B_j(x)(t^j-t^{-j}),
\]
where the \(B_j\) are real regular functions. On the octahedral quotient suppose every effective left-circle weight in each \(B_j\) is divisible by six. On the icosahedral quotient suppose every weight is congruent to \(0,3\), or \(12\) modulo fifteen. Then every nonzero \(P\) has infinitely many nonzero pure moments. All angular coefficients are allowed, with arbitrary polynomial degree. More precisely, at each angular extremal orbit used in the proof, of stabilizer order \(h\), it suffices that the weights avoid the residues \(1\) and \(-1\) modulo \(h\).

First consider a compact probability integral with two smooth normal coordinates \(y\) at each of finitely many maxima of an angular center \(A\), and compact angle variables \(\vartheta\) of dimension at most two. Assume the maxima of \(A\) are nondegenerate and that the imaginary part \(V(y,\vartheta)\) of the complex phase on the integration cycle is stationary in the normal coordinates, at every point above these maxima. For the displayed Laurent phase on the scalar unit circle, \(V=2\sum_j B_j\sin(j\theta)\) and hence is odd in \(\theta\). The normalized trace has the form of the probability integral of \(A+iV\). If the positive moments vanish eventually, its Cauchy transform \(I(z)\) is a polynomial in \(1/z\) whose \(1/z\) coefficient is one. Initially the equality holds at large \(z\), and it extends to the part of \(\operatorname{Re}z>a=\max A\) away from \(0\).

Put \(\eta=\max V(0,\vartheta)\) over the maximal fibres and \(z_0=a+i\eta\). If \(a<0\), \(I\) is analytic at zero, already impossible. If \(a=\eta=0\), take \(z=\delta>0\). On a normal chart \(|z-A-iV|^{-1}\le C/(\delta+|y|^2)\); off the maximal fibres the integral is uniformly bounded. Thus \(I(\delta)=O(1+|\log\delta|)\), again impossible for the proposed polynomial. We can therefore take \(z_0\ne0\).

Approach \(z_0\) along \(z=z_0+\delta e^{i\phi}\), with \(\phi<\pi/2\) fixed and close to \(\pi/2\). In a maximal normal chart write
\[
 z-P=s(\vartheta)+y^{\mathsf T}Q_\vartheta y+O(|y|^3),
 \qquad s(\vartheta)=\delta e^{i\phi}+i(\eta-V(0,\vartheta)).
\]
The real part of the symmetric matrix \(Q_\vartheta\) is uniformly positive definite. On a sufficiently small chart, both denominators with and without the cubic error have absolute value bounded below by a positive multiple of \(|s|+|y|^2\). The real part bounds \(|y|^2\), and the triangle inequality then bounds \(|s|\). In the normal integral for \(-I'(z)\), freeze the smooth volume density at \(y=0\) and extend the leading quadratic kernel to the whole normal plane. This gives
\[
                     \frac{\pi\,\rho(0,\vartheta)}
                                  {s(\vartheta)\sqrt{\det Q_\vartheta}},
\]
using the Gaussian integral with \(\operatorname{Re}s>0\). Uniformly in \(\vartheta\), the error is \(O(1+|s|^{-1/2})\): the errors from density and cubic terms are bounded by the integrals of \(C|y|/(|s|+|y|^2)^2\) and \(C|y|^3/(|s|+|y|^2)^3\), respectively. The part away from the maximal charts is uniformly bounded.

The arguments of the inverse Gaussian square roots lie in a compact subinterval of \((-\pi/2,\pi/2)\), because there are two normal dimensions and the matrices vary over compact sets. Since \(\arg s\in[\phi,\pi/2]\), choose \(\phi\) close enough to \(\pi/2\) that the real part of \(i/[s\sqrt{\det Q_\vartheta}]\) has a uniform positive lower bound times \(1/|s|\). This puts every leading contribution in the same open half-plane. The smaller error can be absorbed using \(x^{-1/2}\le\epsilon x^{-1}+C_\epsilon\). At a maximizing angle on one fibre, smoothness gives \(\eta-V(0,\vartheta)=O(|\vartheta-\vartheta_0|^2)\). The integral of \(1/|s|\) diverges in one or two angle dimensions as \(\delta\downarrow0\). Hence the rotated real part of \(-I'(z)\) is unbounded, whereas the derivative of the asserted Cauchy polynomial is bounded at \(z_0\ne0\). This proves the stationary-fibre test. The same argument applies at a nondegenerate minimum by negating the phase.

Here is why the test applies to the affine centers. On the octahedral sphere the earlier affine-center calculation gives a choice of extremal side on which all extrema are nondegenerate rotation axes. For the icosahedral sphere set \(r=h+7p\) on the unit sphere. The extrema of \(p\) are the vertex and face axes with \((p,r)=(-1/5,1/25)\) and \((1/27,25/81)\), respectively. Let \(\ell(p)\) be the line interpolating those two points. On the mirror \(x_1=0\), set \(b=x_2^2\),
\[
 b_0=\frac12-\frac{\sqrt5}{10},\qquad
 b_1=\frac12+\frac{\sqrt5}{6}.
\]
Direct substitution in the two invariants gives
\[
       r-\ell(p)
       =-12\sqrt5\,(b-b_0)^2(b-b_1)^2
                       \left(b-\frac12+\frac{3\sqrt5}{10}\right).
\]
This is nonpositive on the entire mirror and vanishes only at the two indicated axis orbits. All mirrors are conjugate, and no nonconstant affine combination of \(p,r\) can have a critical point off the mirrors, by the nonzero invariant Jacobian. Thus \(r\le\ell(p)\) everywhere. For center \(\alpha p+\beta r+\gamma\) with \(\beta>0\), compare to its affine upper bound \(\alpha p+\beta\ell(p)+\gamma\) on the interval of \(p\)-values. Every maximum occurs at the indicated vertex or face orbit, with both possible at the tied slope. Along the mirror the defect \(r-\ell(p)\) has nondegenerate maxima at both. The axis stabilizers of orders three and five force the tangent Hessian to be scalar, so the defect has a negative definite tangent Hessian. The affine upper bound has negative semidefinite tangent Hessian at any maximizing endpoint. Hence the center maxima are nondegenerate. Use negation when \(\beta<0\) and the known extrema of \(p\) when \(\beta=0\).

For full left-circle dependence use the angular bundle \(\mathrm{SO}(3)\to S^2\) before the finite right quotient, and integrate in the two angles from the left circle and \(t\). Near a rotation axis with stabilizer of order \(h\), right multiplication by its generator acts on an exponential normal slice by the corresponding planar rotation and shifts the left circle by an element of order \(h\). For a coefficient of effective left-circle weight \(k\), the normal differential at the axis intertwines the planar rotation with the corresponding cyclic character of weight \(k\), up to inversion according to the choice of generator. Over the complex numbers the two characters of the normal plane have weights \(1\) and \(-1\) modulo \(h\). The differential therefore vanishes unless \(k\equiv\pm1\pmod h\). The possible axis orders in the octahedral case are two, three, and four. Avoiding the indicated residues for all three orders is equivalent to divisibility by six. In the icosahedral case the orders on the selected side are three and five, giving the stated three residues modulo fifteen. This proves normal stationarity. With a constant center the Cauchy transform is that of a measure on one vertical segment; eventual vanishing requires a pole at zero, which lies off that segment unless the center is zero. At zero center, even powers of the imaginary part have nonzero trace unless the phase vanishes. For each fixed degree, Laurent support, and asserted start of vanishing, the equations are equivalent to finitely many moment identities by the Hilbert basis theorem. The Fourier restrictions and the nonzero phase condition are algebraic in the coefficients. Real closed field transfer therefore gives the same conclusion over all real closed fields.


## Weighted moments at a conjugate saddle level

**Theorem (A saddle with conjugate branches forces logarithmic weighted densities).**

Let \(X=\mathrm{SO}(3)/\Gamma\) for a finite rotation group \(\Gamma\), with its compact probability trace. Fix a unit vector \(e\), and use the angular projection induced by \(g\mapsto g^{-1}e\). Let \(f\) be a real regular invariant on the sphere. Suppose the critical points at a value \(c\) form a single \(\Gamma\)-orbit of nondegenerate saddles. At one saddle suppose that the two local branches of \(f=c\) lie on complex algebraic curve components in the same \(\Gamma\)-orbit, allowing one component to contain both. For every nonzero real regular \(D\) on \(X\) and every \(M\ge1\), there are arbitrarily large \(m\) divisible by \(2M\) for which the pushforward of \(D^m\) times compact probability by \(f\) has a logarithmic term at \(c\). In particular these weights do not give polynomial or piecewise polynomial densities at all orders. The weight \(D\) may depend on the full quotient.

Work on \(\mathrm{SO}(3)\) before taking the finite quotient. The map to the sphere is a torsor for the left circle, also algebraically after complexification. Over any irreducible complex component \(C\) of \(f=c\) its preimage is irreducible. Indeed the map is smooth with geometrically integral fibres, so every component of the preimage dominates \(C\), and the geometric generic fibre is integral. Invariance under the right \(\Gamma\)-action therefore gives the same divisor order \(b\) of the pullback of \(D\) at the preimages of the components containing the two branches.

At the saddle choose real analytic Morse coordinates \((u,v)\) with \(f=c+uv\), and a real analytic local section of the circle bundle. With \(Z\) the unit-circle coordinate on the fibre, write \(D=(uv)^b D_0(u,v,Z)\) in these coordinates. The division is analytic on a neighborhood of the whole fibre. To see this, expand in the finitely many left-circle weights of the regular function. Each coefficient is analytic in \((u,v)\), and divisibility by \(u^b\) and by \(v^b\) holds at generic points of the respective branches, hence on each branch by analytic continuation. Maximality of the common divisor order ensures that each restriction \(D_0(0,v,Z)\), \(D_0(u,0,Z)\) is nonzero as a germ. Write
\[
             D_0=\sum_{i,j,k} a_{ijk}u^i v^j Z^k,\qquad i,j\ge0.
\]
For each \(i,j\), the coefficients for \(k,-k\) are conjugate. Haar probability locally is a positive analytic density \(\rho(u,v)\,du\,dv\) times normalized uniform circle measure. The density is independent of the circle variable.

Consider the lower Newton polygon of the pairs \((i,j)\) that occur in \(D_0\), taking the convex hull after adding the nonnegative quadrant. The first intersection of this polygon with the diagonal is \((\eta,\eta)\). A supporting line there can have a normal \((\alpha,\beta)\) with both coordinates positive. If \(\eta>0\), this follows because the polygon meets both coordinate axes, which rules out a horizontal or vertical supporting line at that point. If \(\eta=0\), use the face at the origin. Let \(H(Y,Z)\) be the Laurent polynomial formed by the terms on the selected face, replacing \(u^iv^j\) by \(Y^{i-j}\). This substitution is injective on the face. Its Newton polytope contains the origin because the projection of the face meets \(i-j=0\) and all Fourier supports for a fixed \((i,j)\) are symmetric under \(k\mapsto-k\). The Laurent constant-term theorem, applied also to \(H^{2M}\), gives nonzero constant terms of \(H^m\) at arbitrarily large multiples of \(2M\). To obtain arbitrarily large orders, apply the theorem to powers by arbitrarily large multiples of \(2M\). The Newton polytope of a power is the corresponding dilation. [Duistermaat and van der Kallen, *Constant terms in powers of a Laurent polynomial*](https://webspace.science.uu.nl/~kalle101/powers.pdf)

The circle average of \(D_0^m\rho\) has no diagonal monomials \(u^iv^i\) with \(i<m\eta\). At \(i=m\eta\) the diagonal coefficient is \(\rho(0,0)\operatorname{CT}_{Y,Z}H^m\). Positivity of the supporting normal means that only terms on the face and the constant of \(\rho\) can contribute there. A nonzero constant term also implies \(m\eta\) is an integer. Restoring the factor \((uv)^{bm}\) shifts the first nonzero diagonal term to index \(bm+m\eta\).

On a small square \(|u|,|v|<\epsilon\), the pushforward density at \(c+w\), for \(w>0\), is the integral of the circle-averaged density at \((u,w/u)\) with \(du/|u|\) over \(w/\epsilon<|u|<\epsilon\). A diagonal monomial \(u^iv^i\) contributes \(2w^i\log(\epsilon^2/w)\). An off-diagonal monomial \(u^iv^j\) contributes a linear combination of \(w^i\) and \(w^j\), whose coefficients arise by taking the two endpoint values of \(u^{i-j}/(i-j)\). The convergent power series for the density gives convergent analytic series for both the logarithmic coefficient and the part without the logarithm, by choosing \(\epsilon\) within the analytic radius. The first nonzero diagonal term thus gives a nonzero logarithmic term. Choose the saddle squares by transporting one square along the orbit. The contributions agree because the height, weight, and Haar measure are invariant. Away from the saddles the height is a submersion near \(c\). The contribution from that region is smooth at \(c\), including the square boundaries, where the level curves meet the edges transversally for \(|w|\) small. It cannot cancel the logarithmic term.

**Corollary (Nonlinear exceptional heights with the same saddle level).**

For the octahedral quotient let \(p=p_2\) and \(c=1/4\). For the icosahedral quotient let \(p\) be the vertex product in (H4) and set \(c=0\). In either case let \(R\) be any real regular invariant on the angular sphere that is nowhere zero on the real sphere, and set
\[
                              f=c+(p-c)R.
\]
For every nonzero regular real weight on the full corresponding compact quotient, the conclusion of the saddle theorem holds. This includes nonlinear heights, for example \(R=1+p^2+q^2\) in the octahedral case and \(R=1+p^2+h^2\) in the icosahedral case.

In the octahedral case the level \(p=1/4\) is cut out on the unit sphere by the product
\[
                     (x+y+z)(-x+y+z)(x-y+z)(x+y-z).
\]
Indeed the negative of the product is \((x^2+y^2+z^2)^2-4p\). The four planes are conjugate, no three have a common line, and their pair intersections give the twelve edge-axis points, in one orbit. At every intersection the two factors restrict to transverse local coordinates on the sphere, and the other factors are nonzero. In the icosahedral case the six vertex planes are conjugate and no three have a common line. For unit normals to three different vertex axes the Gram determinant is \(1-3/5\pm2/(5\sqrt5)>0\), using their absolute mutual inner products \(1/\sqrt5\). The pair intersections thus give thirty points, all in the orbit of a coordinate-axis point, whose stabilizer has order two. The stabilizer contains the half-turn about that axis, and any nonidentity rotation preserving the saddle Hessian has order two. In both cases the intersection of any individual plane with the complex unit sphere is a smooth irreducible affine conic. Replacing \(p-c\) by \((p-c)R\) preserves the real level, its two local branches, and saddle nondegeneracy, since \(R\) is nonzero there. The height and weight meet all the hypotheses of the theorem.


## A boundary trace on the rank-three double quotient

**Proposition (The spectral boundary trace is an interval and sphere integral).**

Use \(s,u_1,u_2,v_1,v_2\) from the uniform volume trace on the rank-three odd-sign special-unitary quotient. For every polynomial \(F\) in these five regular generators,
\[
\begin{split}
 \Lambda(\partial_s F)={}&360\int_0^1\!\int_0^1 a(1-a)(a-b)\\[-2pt]
 &\quad{}\times
 \mathbb E_{\theta,\psi}
 F\big(2a+b,\ i a\sqrt b\cos\theta,\ i a\sqrt b\sin\theta,\
                i(1-a)\sqrt{1-b}\cos\psi,\ i(1-a)\sqrt{1-b}\sin\psi\big)
 \,db\,da .
\end{split}
\]
Here \(\partial_s\) fixes the four determinant components and the angles are independently uniform. The three variables \(b,\theta,\psi\) give the usual uniform Hopf coordinates on \(S^3\). Thus every polynomial moment on this subring is an integral over one interval and a three-sphere with a fixed signed polynomial weight, after taking an antiderivative in \(s\).

For fixed positive \(N,M\), the repeated-root boundary gives the interval of spectral sums and its orientation. A boundary with positive \(N,M\) has repeated spectrum \((a,a,b)\) with \(0<a,b<1\), hence
\[
              N=a^2b,\qquad M=(1-a)^2(1-b),\qquad s=2a+b.
\]
For \(N\in(0,1)\) fixed, the function \((1-a)^2(1-N/a^2)\) on \((\sqrt N,1)\) has a unique maximum at \(a=N^{1/3}\) and vanishes at both ends. This follows by differentiating, with derivative \(2(1-a)(N/a^3-1)\). Thus there are exactly two candidate boundaries for \(0<M<(1-N^{1/3})^3\), and they are distinct because the corresponding cubic cannot have two distinct double roots. The smaller repeated pair \(a<b\) gives the lower boundary, because increasing \(s\) raises the cubic on \((0,1)\) and creates two roots at its local maximum. The larger repeated pair \(a>b\) gives the upper boundary in the same way at its local minimum. These endpoint classifications and the absence of any other boundary prove the interval assertion.

The uniform volume law in \((s,N,M)\) has density \(180\) after conditional angle averaging. Integrating \(\partial_s F\) gives its upper boundary value minus its lower boundary value. The absolute Jacobian of \((a,b)\mapsto(N,M)\) is \(2a(1-a)|a-b|\), which with the boundary signs gives the displayed formula. The statement on the Hopf variables follows because the squared modulus of either complex coordinate under uniform \(S^3\) has uniform distribution. The identity transfers as a polynomial moment formula over real closed fields by integrating the angular characters and the interval monomials.


### Extending the finite test for the rank-three twist

**Proposition (Six moments exclude affine norm centers with linear determinant parts).**

On the same quotient without a scalar, let the real regular phase \(P\) be affine-linear in \(s,N,M,u_1,u_2,v_1,v_2\), where \(N=-(u_1^2+u_2^2)\) and \(M=-(v_1^2+v_2^2)\) as real regular functions. If the first six moments vanish, then \(P=0\). Over the compact reference the norms \(N,M\) agree with \(\prod k_j,\prod(1-k_j)\), respectively. Compared with the earlier affine test in the five generators, this allows two independent quadratic terms in the determinant arms.

After the first moment the real center is \(aX+bY+cZ\) with \(X=s-3/2,\ Y=N-M,\ Z=N+M-1/7\). Rotate each determinant angle to express the compact-reference imaginary part as
\[
          i\big(\sqrt{(g+h)N}\cos\theta+\sqrt{(g-h)M}\cos\psi\big).
\]
Thus \(g+h,g-h\) are the squared lengths of the two real coefficient vectors. Expand each moment using the independent circle formulas \(\mathbb E(\cos^{2j}\theta)=4^{-j}\binom{2j}{j}\) and \(\mathbb E(\cos^{2j+1}\theta)=0\). For the spectral terms use the ordered density \(180(y-x)(z-x)(z-y)\) with the simplex monomial integral \(1/[(i+1)(i+j+2)(i+j+k+3)]\) on \(x^iy^jz^k\). In particular the second moment gives
\[
              g=\frac32a^2+\frac65ab+\frac4{15}b^2+\frac4{35}c^2 .
\]
If \(9a+4b=0\), the third moment becomes \(-c(4112c^2+28077a^2)/9055200\), forcing \(c=0\). At \(c=0\) the fourth moment is \((2402400h^2+123091a^4)/448448000\), forcing \(h=a=0\). All coefficients are then zero by the second moment.

If \(9a+4b\ne0\), the third moment gives
\[
       h=\frac{2c(8085a^2+11319ab+3087b^2-257c^2)}
                    {8085(9a+4b)} .
\]
After the two substitutions, the even moments depend on \(c^2\), and the fifth moment is \(c\) times a rational function of \(c^2\). For the parity, replacing (c) by (-c) sends the substituted (h) to (-h). Complementing the spectral roots, exchanging the determinant angles, and reversing both cosine signs then changes the phase to its negative.

Scale to \(a=1\), or to \(a=0,b=1\). Put \(r=c^2\). For \(c\ne0\), the numerators of the fourth, fifth divided by \(c\), and sixth moments generate the unit ideal after imposing the nonzero denominator, using an inverse variable for \(9+4b\) in the first scale. For \(c=0\) the same holds with \(r=0\) and just the fourth and sixth moments. These four exact computations are replayed in the [moment checker](verify_rank_three_radial_affine_moments.py), which builds the moments by the displayed integrals and computes the four rational Gröbner bases. They prove the exclusion in this branch over any field of characteristic zero. The singular branch uses only sums of real squares. The complete conclusion therefore holds over every real closed field.


## Regular angular fibrations with at most two points at infinity

**Theorem (A regular angular circle fibration requires an invariant axis).**

Let \(K\) be real closed, let \(Q=\{x_1^2+x_2^2+x_3^2=1\}\), and let \(\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SO}(3,K)\) be a finite rotation group. There exists a nonconstant regular height on \(Q/\Gamma\) whose geometric generic fibre has a component isomorphic to \(\mathbb G_m\) if and only if \(\Gamma\) preserves an unoriented line. No nonconstant regular height on \(Q/\Gamma\) has a geometric generic fibre component isomorphic to \(\mathbb A^1\). For either exceptional angular quotient this excludes a regular height with a conditional conic having two points at infinity, independently of the choice of generators.

Work first over \(\mathbb R\). Let \(f\) be the proposed height. On the complex affine sphere a nonidentity element of \(\Gamma\) has at most two fixed points, because its fixed space is its axis. Thus the finite quotient map is étale away from finitely many points in the quotient. Restricting it to the geometric generic fibre of \(f\) gives a finite étale cover. The components of the geometric generic fibre are conjugate over the height field since the quotient is integral. Each component of the geometric fibre upstairs is therefore of the same proposed type, by the classification of connected finite étale covers of \(\mathbb A^1\) and \(\mathbb G_m\) in characteristic zero. For \(\mathbb A^1\), completion and Riemann–Hurwitz force degree one. For \(\mathbb G_m\) they force a projective line with exactly one point above each of the two punctures. [Vakil and Wickelgren, §3.12](https://services.math.duke.edu/~kgw/papers/VW.pdf)

Let \(L\) be the relative algebraic closure of \(\mathbb C(f)\) in the function field of the complex sphere. It is a finite extension. Let \(B\) be the normalization of the affine height line in \(L\). Its coordinate ring lies in the coordinate ring of \(Q_{\mathbb C}\), which is integrally closed. Its smooth projective completion is rational. Indeed the sphere is rational, and restricting a dominant rational map from that surface to the completed curve to a suitable line in a rational chart gives a nonconstant rational map from \(\mathbb P^1\) to the curve. The only units of \(Q_{\mathbb C}\) are constants, since the boundary of its smooth projective quadric completion is one irreducible ample conic. The divisor of a unit would be a multiple of that conic, and intersection with a hyperplane forces that multiple to be zero. The curve \(B\) is affine, and deleting two or more points from its projective line would give nonconstant units. Thus \(B\) is an affine line. This normalization descends by conjugation, and the one point at infinity is real. The descended genus-zero completion is therefore a real projective line. In particular \(B\) has a real affine coordinate \(g\), giving a real regular function on \(Q\).

The group \(\Gamma\) acts on \(B\). A finite group of real affine transformations has a fixed point by averaging, and, after translating that point to zero, it acts by signs. Thus the kernel \(\Gamma_0\) of its action on \(g\) has index at most two. By the definition of \(L\) and characteristic zero, the generic fibre of \(g\) is geometrically integral and gives one of the geometric generic components upstairs. Its isomorphism to \(\mathbb A^1\) or \(\mathbb G_m\) is defined over a finite extension of the function field of \(B\). Spreading out the isomorphism and its inverse over a nonempty open of the normalization of \(B\) in that extension shows that outside finitely many values all geometric fibres have the same type.

The real function \(g\) is nonconstant on the real sphere by Zariski density. Its image contains an interval. Choose a value in that interval for which the geometric fibre has the stated type. Its real locus is nonempty and compact. Its smooth projective completion is a projective line over \(\mathbb R\), since it has a real point. Compactness forces every deleted point to be nonreal, since deleting a real point from this real projective line would leave its real locus nonclosed. For the one-puncture case this is impossible. In the two-puncture case the real locus is a single circle.

The group \(\Gamma_0\) preserves the orientation of this level circle induced by the orientation of the sphere and the direction of increasing \(g\). It acts faithfully, since a nonidentity rotation cannot fix a circle pointwise. Every finite faithful group of orientation-preserving circle transformations is cyclic. A nonidentity element of finite order cannot fix a point, by passing to the interval cut there and using monotonicity. Thus the action on an orbit embeds the group in the cyclic permutations of that orbit. It follows that \(\Gamma_0\) is cyclic. A nontrivial normal cyclic subgroup has a unique rotation axis, preserved as an unoriented line by all of \(\Gamma\). If \(\Gamma_0\) is trivial then \(|\Gamma|\le2\), which also supplies an invariant line.

For the converse choose a unit vector \(e\) on an invariant line and use \(f=e\cdot x\) if the group fixes \(e\), or \(f=(e\cdot x)^2\) if it acts by both signs. Before the quotient the geometric generic latitude is a conic with two points removed. In the squared case there are two such latitudes, exchanged by any element that reverses \(e\). The stabilizer of one latitude acts by a finite group of rotations about the axis. Its quotient of \(\mathbb G_m\) is again \(\mathbb G_m\), as seen by taking the corresponding power of an angular coordinate. This proves the assertion over \(\mathbb R\). For transfer, a proposed counterexample can be described by finite algebraic data. Over a finite extension of the height field all components split and are isomorphic to the claimed standard curves. After choosing a presentation of that extension, the isomorphisms and inverse are given by polynomial or rational formulas of finite degree with nonzero denominators. Clearing denominators in their defining equations gives finitely many polynomial conditions on the coefficients. The group multiplication table and, where required, the absence of an invariant axis are also first-order conditions over a real closed field. Real closed field transfer would give such data over \(\mathbb R\), where the result just proved forbids them. The converse is algebraic over every real closed field.


## Piecewise polynomial densities of a single left-circle harmonic

**Theorem (A piecewise polynomial single-harmonic law requires an invariant axis).**

Let \(\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SO}(3,\mathbb R)\) be a finite rotation group, \(X=\mathrm{SO}(3)/\Gamma\), and \(L\ne0\) a complex regular function of a single nonzero weight for a left maximal circle. The law of \(\operatorname{Re}L\) under compact probability can have a piecewise polynomial density with finitely many pieces only if \(\Gamma\) preserves an unoriented axis. This remains necessary after multiplying the Haar measure by a continuous left-circle invariant weight that is strictly positive on the compact quotient. In particular no such height and weight can supply a piecewise-polynomial interval construction on either full exceptional quotient.

Let \(R=L\overline L\), viewed as a real analytic invariant on the angular sphere. The weight becomes a function on the sphere that is bounded above and bounded below by a positive constant. Let \(\nu\) be the corresponding weighted pushforward under \(R\), let \(M=\max R>0\), and let \(d\) be the density of \(\operatorname{Re}L\). At fixed angular point the circle acts by a uniform phase, giving the Abel relations
\[
 d(u)=\frac1\pi\int_{r>u^2}\frac{d\nu(r)}{\sqrt{r-u^2}},
 \qquad
 \nu((r,M])=\int_r^M\frac{d(\sqrt z)}{\sqrt{z-r}}\,dz
 \quad(0<r<M).
\]
The second follows from the first by Fubini and the beta integral with parameters \(1/2,1/2\). If \(R\) is constant, \(d\) is the arcsine density with an unbounded singularity at its endpoints. Otherwise \(\nu\) has no atoms by analyticity of the nonconstant \(R\).

Suppose \(d\) is piecewise polynomial. Write \(F(z)=d(\sqrt z)\) and integrate the second formula by parts on its finitely many pieces. For \(r>0\) away from the positive knots \(z_j\) and the endpoint \(M\), this gives the density
\[
 \frac{d\nu}{dr}
     =\frac{F(M-)}{\sqrt{M-r}}
       -\int_r^M\frac{F'_{\rm pw}(z)}{\sqrt{z-r}}\,dz
       -\sum_{z_j>r}\frac{F(z_j+)-F(z_j-)}{\sqrt{z_j-r}} .
\]
Here \(F'_{\rm pw}\) is bounded on every compact subinterval away from zero, including the one-sided limits at the knots. In particular, for each fixed \(c>0\),
\[
       \nu([c,c+\epsilon])=O(\epsilon),\qquad
       \nu([c-\epsilon,c])=O(\sqrt\epsilon).
\]
An interval outside the support contributes zero. These are also upper bounds for spherical area of the corresponding level strips, since the weight is bounded below. Boundedness of \(d\) near zero and the first Abel formula give \(\int R^{-1/2}<\infty\) on the angular sphere by Fatou's lemma. Thus \(R^{-1}(0)\) is finite. Otherwise the analytic zero set contains a smooth arc. The modulus \(|L|=\sqrt R\) is at most a constant times the distance to that arc in a local circle-bundle section, which makes the integral diverge.

Here are the resulting constraints on positive critical levels of \(R\). A degenerate local minimum of value \(c>0\) violates the \(O(\epsilon)\) bound above \(c\): at Hessian rank one a rectangle with side lengths proportional to \(\epsilon^{1/2},\epsilon^{1/3}\) lies in the strip by Taylor expansion, and at rank zero a disk of radius proportional to \(\epsilon^{1/3}\) suffices. A critical point \(p\) that is neither a local maximum nor a minimum also violates this bound. Its level has a real analytic arc approaching \(p\) that bounds a sector of \(\{R>c\}\). This follows from the local finite-arc decomposition of the real algebraic level in a sphere chart. On a smooth open subarc either \(dR\ne0\) or the level equation has a fixed normal vanishing order \(k\ge2\) at generic points. In the latter case a strip on the positive side has area at least a constant times \(\epsilon^{1/k}\). In the former, the coarea limit of the positive strip area divided by \(\epsilon\) on a compact subarc is bounded below by a constant times \(\int |dR|^{-1}\,ds\). This integral tends to infinity along subarcs approaching \(p\), since \(|dR|\le C|x-p|\). Thus every positive critical point is a local maximum or a nondegenerate minimum. [Bierstone and Milman, *Semianalytic and subanalytic sets*](https://www.numdam.org/item/PMIHES_1988__67__5_0/)

At a nonisolated local maximum of value \(c>0\), the Hessian must have rank one. If it has rank zero, the local nonnegative function \(c-R\) has zero cubic term, and its Hessian is \(O(|x-p|^2)\). A smooth subarc of the maximal level approaching \(p\) has strips of normal width proportional to \(\sqrt\epsilon/|x-p|\) in the level strip below \(c\), by Taylor expansion. For each fixed compact subarc this estimate holds for all sufficiently small \(\epsilon\). The integral of \(ds/|x-p|\) diverges as the subarc approaches \(p\), contrary to the \(O(\sqrt\epsilon)\) bound. At rank one, the implicit function theorem applied to the derivative in the nondegenerate direction puts any nearby maximum-level points on an analytic graph. The accumulation of such points makes \(R=c\) identically along the graph. Thus the nonisolated maxima are smooth circles with strictly negative transverse Hessian. There are finitely many circles and finitely many isolated critical points, since the critical set of the regular function on the algebraic sphere is semialgebraic.

Cut the sphere along the maximum circles and remove a thin collar at each boundary. On each resulting compact region the gradient of \(R\) points outward near the boundary, by the negative transverse Hessian. Every interior critical point is an isolated extremum, with gradient index \(+1\). This includes the isolated zeros of \(R\). For example a sufficiently small regular level around an isolated extremum bounds a disk enclosing it, since a hole would contain a different critical point. The gradient index on its boundary is one. Every region with boundary contains an interior minimum, because passing inward from any maximum circle decreases \(R\). Poincaré–Hopf now gives \(2-b\ge1\) in a region with \(b\) boundary circles. Thus if there are maximum circles there is exactly one, since cutting along \(k>0\) disjoint circles gives \(k+1\) regions with \(2k\) total boundaries. Each disk then has exactly one critical point, its minimum. The two minima are permuted by \(\Gamma\), so their difference defines an invariant line. If there are no maximum circles, Poincaré–Hopf on the sphere gives exactly two isolated extrema and the unique minimum is fixed by \(\Gamma\). This gives the invariant axis in both cases. [Milnor, *Topology from the Differentiable Viewpoint*, §6](https://www.math.auckland.ac.nz/~hekmati/Books/Milnor.pdf)


## Projector overlap on point-free classical products

**Theorem (Projector overlap gives regular all-order witnesses on twisted products).**

Let \(K\) be any field of characteristic zero. Take central simple algebras with involution \((A_0,\sigma_0),(A_1,\sigma_1)\) of the same degree and type. In unitary type fix a common quadratic étale center and require isomorphisms to preserve it. Let \(X\) be a geometrically connected \(K\)-isomorphism torsor of the two algebras with involution, using a specified connected component in orthogonal type. Let \(p_0,p_1\) be nontrivial self-adjoint idempotents in \(A_0,A_1\), respectively. Take any reductive source stabilizer \(H\) that preserves \(p_0\) and acts by precomposition on \(X\). Then \(Y=(X/H)\times\mathbb G_m\) has a regular phase and one regular Laurent unit multiplier with nonzero marked moments at every positive order in the following cases:

- Unitary or symplectic type, with arbitrary nontrivial idempotent ranks.
- Orthogonal type in even degree, with the two reduced ranks of opposite parity.

The result applies when \(X/H\) has no ground-field point and when the acting group has no ground-field long-root subgroup.

In the unitary and orthogonal cases use the ambient degree \(n\) and the two reduced ranks. In the symplectic case use half the degree and half the two reduced ranks, so that \(n\) and the ranks measure quaternionic dimensions in the compact reference. Complement either idempotent as necessary, and call the ordered smaller and larger ranks \(a,b\), with \(1\le a\le b\) and \(a+b\le n\). Complementation preserves the original stabilizer. For \(\varphi\in X\), take
\[
       s=\frac{1}{d_0}\operatorname{Trd}_{A_1}\bigl(p_1\varphi(p_0)\bigr),
       \qquad
       d_0=\begin{cases}
               2 &\text{in symplectic type},\\
               1 &\text{otherwise}.
             \end{cases}
\]
In the unitary case the trace is fixed by center conjugation, since the involution reverses the two self-adjoint factors and reduced trace is cyclic. Thus \(s\) is regular over \(K\), and it is \(H\)-invariant. Put \(x=s/a\).

Over the geometric field identify both idempotents with orthogonal projectors in the compact reference for the normalized trace. One can choose the two matrix identifications separately, sending each self-adjoint idempotent to its standard coordinate block. The trace of \(\varphi\mapsto g\) is then compact Haar on the corresponding projective group. The variable \(s\) is the sum of the squared principal cosines for the two subspaces, one Haar translated. Its law is unchanged by interchanging their roles, since the inverse of a Haar element has the same law. In orthogonal type passing to the connected compact group leaves the Grassmannian law unchanged. In unitary type the central circle also leaves the projectors unchanged. Define \(\beta=1,2,4\) in orthogonal, unitary, and symplectic type, respectively. The ordered principal cosines squared have density, up to a positive normalizing constant,
\[
          \prod_{i<j}(k_j-k_i)^\beta
             \prod_{i=1}^a k_i^\alpha(1-k_i)^\gamma,\qquad
          0<k_1<\cdots<k_a<1,
          \quad
          \alpha=\frac{\beta}{2}(b-a+1)-1,\quad
          \gamma=\frac{\beta}{2}(n-b-a+1)-1 .
\]
These are the compact BDI, AIII, and CII Jacobi laws for the indicated rectangular blocks. [Edelman and Jeong, §§6.1–6.3](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2011.08087)

Under the given parity hypotheses \(\alpha,\gamma\) are nonnegative integers. The normalized joint law is therefore a polynomial density on the ordered simplex, with rational coefficients. To compute the density of \(s=\sum k_i\), slice this simplex by its height. Its vertex heights are \(0,1,\ldots,a\). Between consecutive heights the slice vertices vary affinely along fixed simplex edges and its face lattice is constant. A fixed pulling triangulation of the slice gives polynomial volume integrals, using an affine coordinate system that eliminates one \(k_i\) in favor of \(s\). Integrating a polynomial of degree \(a(\alpha+\gamma)+\beta\binom a2\) on an \((a-1)\)-simplex with affine-moving vertices gives degree at most
\[
                         B=a(\alpha+\gamma)+\beta\binom a2+a-1.
\]
Signs of the simplex volume determinants are constant on each open piece. It follows that \(x\) has a rational piecewise polynomial probability density, with possible knots \(j/a\). At \(x=1\), put \(h_i=1-k_i\). The leading density of the \(h_i\) at the corner is a positive homogeneous polynomial of degree \(a\gamma+\beta\binom a2\) on the ordered region. Slicing \(\sum h_i=a(1-x)\) gives right endpoint vanishing order
\[
                     q=a\gamma+\beta\binom a2+a-1
                       =\frac{\beta}{2}a(n-b)-1.
\]
The inward derivative of order \(q\), denoted \(A_q\), is positive and rational.

Set \(L=B+1\), \(T=1+t\), and take
\[
 f(u)=(1-u)\prod_{j=1}^{a-1}(1-au/j)^2,\qquad
 p=Tt^{-2}f(Tx)^2,\qquad P=p^L(1+t^Lp),\qquad Q=t^{-(q+1)} .
\]
Here the product has value \(1\) if \(a=1\). The polynomial \(f\) has the required double zeros at all positive interior knots and \(f'(1)=-1\). The [piecewise-polynomial endpoint criterion](PIECEWISE_DENSITY_AND_COMPACT_COADJOINT_ALL_ORDER_COUNTEREXAMPLES.md) gives the all-order identities
\[
               \Lambda_Y(P^m)=0,\qquad
               \Lambda_Y(QP^m)=A_q\,\frac{(2Lm)!}{(2Lm+q+1)!}
               \qquad(m\ge1).
\]
These are identities in rational moments in the compact reference. They hold over \(K\) by algebraic base change of normalized trace. More explicitly, after geometric scalar extension the projector model is the standard one and is defined over the algebraic numbers, so comparison with compact Haar requires no embedding of \(K\) into \(\mathbb R\). Returning from the torsor to the \(H\)-quotient preserves the trace on the invariant test functions by invariance and normalization.


### An index-four example beyond the long-root hypothesis

Take a characteristic-zero rational function field in nine independent variables, with the ninth written \(\varepsilon\). For \(i=0,1\) let \(D_i\) be the tensor of two quaternion symbols on its own four of the first eight variables, and set \(A_i=M_2(D_i)\). The two division algebras have index four and distinct Brauer classes. Indeed, embed the rational function field into the corresponding iterated Laurent series field. The monomial basis for any tensor product of these independent quaternion symbols has distinct values modulo the integral value group, by assigning half of each parameter value to the respective symbol generator. A product of two nonzero elements has a unique smallest term obtained from the smallest terms of its factors. Applied to each \(D_i\) and to the tensor of both, this proves the division and Brauer assertions.

Give each \(D_i\) a symplectic involution \(\tau_i\) by tensoring the canonical involution of its first quaternion with an orthogonal involution of its second. On \(A_i\) take the involution adjoint to the diagonal \(\tau_i\)-Hermitian form \(\langle1,\varepsilon\rangle\). The diagonal projector of one copy of \(D_i\) is self-adjoint of reduced rank four. The form is anisotropic: in the \(\varepsilon\)-adic Laurent series division algebra over the first-eight-variable field, the two nonzero terms \(\tau_i(u)u\) and \(\varepsilon\tau_i(v)v\) have valuations of opposite parity. The isometry group is therefore anisotropic, since the value of a nontrivial split torus weight vector in a Hermitian form would be zero, and passing to the adjoint group preserves split rank. The isomorphism torsor \(X\) has no \(K\)-point because the Brauer classes differ. For this example take \(H=1\). The adjoint target in particular has no \(K\)-defined long-root subgroup, also directly for an index reason. If it did, the subgroup would lift isomorphically to the simply connected symplectic group, since the geometric long-root subgroup in rank four misses the center of the full group. Its nonidentity central involution \(j\) would yield the self-adjoint idempotent \((1-j)/2\) of reduced rank two. This contradicts index four.

Here \(n=4\), \(a=b=2\), \(L=10\) and \(q+1=8\). The density in ordered coordinates is \(2800(y-x)^4xy(1-x)(1-y)\). Its normalizing integral before multiplying by \(2800\) is \(1/2800\). The leading coefficient of \((1-(x+y)/2)^7\) in the normalized height density is \(2^8\cdot2800/140\), since the endpoint slice has integral \(\int_0^{1/2}(1-2u)^4u(1-u)\,du=1/140\). Thus \(A_q=25804800\), and the single marker \(t^{-8}\) has marked moments \(25804800(20m)!/(20m+8)!\) at every \(m\ge1\).


### The complex-structure projector in every quaternionic rank

**Corollary (All-order product witnesses for symplectic complex-structure quotients).**

In the symplectic case of the torsor theorem, let the common degree be \(2n\). Suppose \(A_i\) contain \(J_i\) satisfying \(J_i^2=-1,\ \sigma_i(J_i)=-J_i\), and \(H\) centralizes the source \(J_0\). Then \((X/H)\times\mathbb G_m\) has a regular all-order witness over \(K\). By pullback along the equivariant conjugation map from an isometry torsor to the associated algebra-with-involution torsor, this includes the standard quaternionic sign torsors with full special-unitary stabilizer in every rank.

Use the regular height
\[
                 s=\frac14\operatorname{Trd}_{A_1}
                                     (1-J_1\varphi(J_0)),\qquad x=s/n .
\]
After adjoining a square root \(i\) of \(-1\), this is the overlap trace of the two complementary-Lagrangian projectors \((1-J_1/i)/2\) and \((1-\varphi(J_0)/i)/2\). Indeed the reduced traces of the two skew elements are zero. The displayed expression descends to \(K\) and is \(H\)-invariant. Over the geometric field the two \(J_i\) are the standard complex structures in the compact reference. The squared cosines have the type CI law \(\prod_{i<j}(k_j-k_i)\) on the ordered unit simplex, as in the rank-three calculation. The same simplex and corner arguments give rational polynomial pieces for \(x\) with knots \(j/n\), and degree bound and right endpoint order both \(n(n+1)/2-1\). Use the previous phase with \(a=n\), \(L=n(n+1)/2\) and \(Q=t^{-L}\). The compact trace formula and base change give the fixed nonzero marked moments at all orders.


## Two circle averages at a vanishing imaginary amplitude

**Theorem (A single active harmonic at a zero forces nonvanishing).**

Let \(X\) be a pointed compact real rotation quotient \(\mathrm{SO}(3)/\Gamma\), with a left circle and its angular sphere quotient. Use Haar probability and split scalar constant term on \(X\times\mathbb G_m\). The following nonzero real regular phases have infinitely many nonzero pure moments.

1. Write
\[
 P=A_0+\sum_j A_j(t^j+t^{-j})
                +\sum_j(\operatorname{Re} L_j)(t^j-t^{-j}),
\]
where the \(A_j\) are angular and the complex \(L_j\) have one common nonzero left-circle weight. Suppose the real center \(S(x,\theta)=A_0(x)+2\sum_j A_j(x)\cos(j\theta)\) has a global maximum or minimum at \(\theta=0\) or \(\theta=\pi\). The result also holds at any fixed \(\theta_0\) where the imaginary amplitude vanishes for every angular point near the contact. In particular an arbitrary angular center \(A_0\) with no symmetric scalar terms needs no additional condition.
2. Write \(P=A+B(t^k-t^{-k})\) with \(k\ge1\), \(A\) angular and \(B\) real. Suppose \(B\) has a zero on every left-circle fibre over a neighborhood of some global maximum or minimum of \(A\). This holds in particular if the left-circle average of \(B\) is zero there, and allows arbitrary left-circle weights in \(B\).

The two conclusions apply over every real closed field in the regular compact models. They include both full exceptional quotients, with no degree bound on the angular center or the indicated coefficients.

Work on \(\mathrm{SO}(3)\) before the finite quotient, with \(x\) on the angular sphere and the left phase denoted by \(\psi\). Haar disintegrates into sphere area and a uniform angle in any local section. In the first case put \(t=e^{i\theta}\) and average \(\psi\) first. The imaginary part of \(P\) is
\[
       2\operatorname{Re}\left(e^{ih\psi}\sum_j \ell_j(x)\sin(j\theta)\right),
\]
with \(h\ne0\) and regularity in local sections. Thus conditionally it has the form \(B_0(x,\theta)\cos\psi\) in law, where \(B_0(x,\theta)=2|\sum_j\ell_j(x)\sin(j\theta)|\). In the second case average \(\theta\) first, with real center \(S=A\) and amplitude \(B_0=2|B|\) depending on \(x,\psi\). The scalar Cauchy integral in either case is
\[
       I(z)=\int \big((z-S)^2+B_0^2\big)^{-1/2}\,d\mu,
                         \qquad z>a=\max S ,
\]
with the remaining angular variables in \(d\mu\) and the positive branch for real \(z\). This identity first holds at large \(z\) by expansion. If all sufficiently large pure moments vanished, \(I\) would equal a polynomial in \(1/z\) with coefficient one at \(1/z\). Real analytic continuation gives the equality for \(z>\max(a,0)\). If \(a<0\), boundedness at zero contradicts the pole. If \(a=0\), then \(0<zI(z)\le1\) and dominated convergence gives \(\lim_{z\downarrow0}zI(z)=\mu\{S=B_0=0\}<1\) for a nonzero phase. Indeed the limit is the integral of the indicator on the right, and full support and continuity would make the phase zero if that measure were one. This again contradicts the Cauchy polynomial, since the bound rules out any higher pole.

For \(a>0\), the integrand of \(-I'(z)\) is nonnegative and equals \((z-S)((z-S)^2+B_0^2)^{-3/2}\). In the first case use a maximizing contact \((x_0,\theta_0)\) as in the hypothesis and put \(p_0=z-S(x,\theta_0)\). For \(x\) near \(x_0\), \(p_0\le z-a+C|x-x_0|^2\) in local sphere coordinates. On an angle interval \(|\theta-\theta_0|<c p_0\) with a uniform small \(c>0\), one has \(z-S\) comparable to \(p_0\) and \(B_0=O(p_0)\), by the bounded derivatives and the assumed zero of the amplitude. The angle integral of \(-I'\) is therefore at least a positive constant times \(1/p_0\). In the second case \(p_0=z-A(x)\) is independent of \(\psi\). On an interval of length proportional to \(p_0\) about any zero of \(B\) on the fibre one has \(|B|=O(p_0)\), again with a uniform constant. This gives the same lower bound after the remaining angle integral. Integrating over the two normal angular coordinates yields a divergent lower bound proportional to
\[
                         \int_{|y|<\epsilon}
                                     \frac{dy}{z-a+C|y|^2}
\]
as \(z\downarrow a\). The derivative of the asserted Cauchy polynomial is bounded at \(a>0\). Negating the phase handles a minimum contact.

For fixed Laurent support and coefficient spaces, eventual vanishing is equivalent to finitely many polynomial equations by the Hilbert basis theorem. The group weights, contact conditions and fibre zero conditions are semialgebraic in such data on the regular quotient models. The same contradiction follows over any real closed field by transfer.


**Corollary (Nonpositive opposite products at fibre zeros).**

The same Cauchy obstruction applies to either of the following phases, allowing the Laurent coefficients to have no reflection symmetry.

- \(P=A+B/t^k+Ct^k\) with real regular coefficients, \(k\ge1\), \(A\) angular, \(BC\not\equiv0\), and \(BC\le0\) on the compact quotient. If \(BC\) has a zero on every left-circle fibre over a neighborhood of a global extremum of \(A\), then \(P\) has infinitely many nonzero pure moments.
- \(P=A+U+V\) with \(A\) angular and \(U,V\) of opposite nonzero left-circle weights, all Laurent in the split scalar and with \(P\) real over the ground field. Suppose on the scalar unit circle that \(A(x,t)\) is real and \(UV(x,t)\) is real and nonpositive. If a global extremum of \(A(x,e^{i\theta})\) occurs at \((x_0,\theta_0)\), where \(UV(x,e^{i\theta_0})=0\) for all \(x\) near \(x_0\), then every nonzero such phase has infinitely many nonzero pure moments.

In the first case take the constant term over the split scalar circle and in the second case take the left-circle projection. The corresponding conditional Cauchy transform in either case is \(\big((z-A)^2-4BC\big)^{-1/2}\) or \(\big((z-A)^2-4UV\big)^{-1/2}\), by the binomial series in opposite weights. Its derivative for \(z>\max A\) has the sign used in the theorem. The square root of the nonnegative angular radius squared is \(O(|\theta-\theta_0|)\) along the remaining circle about a zero, because the first derivative there vanishes and the second derivatives are bounded. The proof of the theorem then applies through the same two-dimensional divergent integral. At a zero global maximum the dominated limit can equal one only if both the center and radius squared vanish identically. This is excluded by \(BC\not\equiv0\) in the first case. In the second case reality identifies \(U,V\) by conjugation on the left circle for real \(t\), so if their product vanishes identically, both do. In that case the putative limit one would force \(P=0\).


## Hermitian matrix coefficients on the special-unitary quotient

**Theorem (Interlacing variables give a uniform trace for the Hermitian block).**

Use the quaternionic sign isometry torsor from \(I_{n-r}\perp-I_r\) to \(I_n\), modulo the full source special unitary group. In the symplectic comparison blocks \(P,Q,R,T\), the matrix \(A=PT^{\mathsf T}\) is right invariant and descends as a Hermitian matrix over \(K(i)/K\). It is the ordinary Hermitian matrix \(PP^*\) on the compact reference. If \(k_1<\cdots<k_n\) are its eigenvalues, the interlacing array of the leading principal submatrices of \(A\) is uniformly distributed on the polytope whose top row ranges over \(0<k_1<\cdots<k_n<1\). Conditional on the array, the two determinant angles are independent uniform angles and their squared moduli are \(N=\prod k_i\) and \(M=\prod(1-k_i)\). In the odd-negative-rank twist the four real determinant components are evaluated as \(i\) times the corresponding compact-reference components. This gives mixed polynomial moments of the diagonal or the leading principal characteristic coefficients with both determinant arms.

Indeed, right multiplication by \(h\in\mathrm{SL}_n\) sends \(P\) to \(Ph^{-1}\) and \(T\) to \(Th^{\mathsf T}\). In the descent by the right sign matrix the signs cancel in \(PT^{\mathsf T}\), and compact conjugation interchanges \(P\) and \(T\), so its individual entries descend with conjugate transpose. The base eigenvalues have the type CI ordered density proportional to \(\prod_{i<j}(k_j-k_i)\). At fixed eigenvalues the matrix orbit is uniform under \(U(n)\)-conjugation. The leading-minor array of this orbit has normalized uniform distribution on the corresponding Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope. Its volume is \(\prod_{i<j}(k_j-k_i)/\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}j!\), so the conditional normalization cancels the Vandermonde from the top row. [Leake, McSwiggen, and Vishnoi, Proposition 2.2](https://www.cs.yale.edu/homes/vishnoi/HCIZ.pdf)

The scalar circle in the left \(U(n)\) and the determinant circle of the right \(U(n)\) preserve \(A\) and rotate \(d_P,d_Q\) independently up to a surjective finite isogeny, giving their independent phases. The sign in the odd-rank descent gives the indicated imaginary components as in the double quotient. Diagonal entries of \(A\) are differences of successive row sums in the array. The full array is a calculation variable, while these differences and the characteristic coefficients of the leading principal minors are regular functions. The resulting polynomial identities transfer by algebraic base change.


### A finite test with arbitrary Hermitian linear center

**Proposition (Six moments exclude Hermitian linear centers with linear determinant parts).**

On the bare rank-three odd-sign quaternionic quotient by the full special-unitary stabilizer, a real phase
\[
           a_0+\operatorname{tr}(C A)
                      +\sum_{i=1}^2 (\alpha_i u_i+\beta_i v_i)
\]
with an arbitrary constant Hermitian matrix \(C\) and real coefficients has a nonzero moment among its first six unless it vanishes. This permits arbitrary off-diagonal Hermitian matrix coefficients. It is independent of the earlier test allowing determinant norms in a spectral-sum center.

Over the compact reference diagonalize \(C\), with eigenvalues \(c_1,c_2,c_3\). Put \(\tau=\operatorname{tr}C\), \(v=\operatorname{tr}((C-\tau I/3)^2)\), and \(w=\operatorname{tr}((C-\tau I/3)^3)\). Also let \(g+h=\|\alpha\|^2,\ g-h=\|\beta\|^2\). The first moment fixes \(a_0=-\tau/2\). In the rank-three array write the top row as \(x<y<z\), the second row as \(l<u\), and the bottom as \(d\). Their joint density is \(360\) on
\[
            0<x<y<z<1,\qquad x<l<y<u<z,\qquad l<d<u.
\]
Indeed the conditional array volume is half the top-row Vandermonde and the top-row normalization is \(180\). Thus the real center is the linear form
\[
            (c_1-c_2)d+(c_2-c_3)(l+u)+c_3(x+y+z)-\tau/2 .
\]
The imaginary part is \(i(\sqrt{(g+h)xyz}\cos\theta+
\sqrt{(g-h)(1-x)(1-y)(1-z)}\cos\psi)\), with independent uniform angles.

Write \(M_j\) for the \(j\)-th moment after the first-moment adjustment. Direct polynomial integration gives
\[
\begin{split}
 M_2&=(-6g+\tau^2+3v)/84,\qquad M_3=-3h\tau/140,\\
 M_4&=(567g^2-396g\tau^2-540gv+243h^2+
            19\tau^4+90\tau^2v-72\tau w+162v^2)/45360 .
\end{split}
\]
The calculation expands the even cosine powers, using \(\mathbb E(\cos^{2j}\theta)=4^{-j}\binom{2j}{j}\). In an array monomial integrate \(d\) from \(l\) to \(u\) first, then \(l\) from \(x\) to \(y\) and \(u\) from \(y\) to \(z\). The remaining ordered simplex integral of \(x^i y^j z^k\) is \(1/[(i+1)(i+j+2)(i+j+k+3)]\). The [exact checker](verify_rank_three_hermitian_centers.py) uses these same integrals to verify the second, third, fourth, and relevant sixth moment formulas.

If \(\tau=0\), vanishing of the second and fourth moments forces \(M_4=(36h^2+5v^2)/6720=0\), so \(C=0\) and \(g=0\). If \(\tau\ne0\), scale to \(\tau=1\). Vanishing of the first four moments gives
\[
                    h=0,\qquad g=(1+3v)/6,\qquad
                    w=(135v^2-414v-125)/288 .
\]
The three real eigenvalues of the traceless matrix \(C-I/3\) give \(v\ge0\) and \(v^3\ge6w^2\), by the discriminant of their characteristic cubic. On \(0\le v\le1\) the proposed value of \(w\) has magnitude at least \(125/288\), contradicting that inequality. For \(v>1\) the sixth moment at the proposed parameters is
\[
             M_6=\frac{14175v^4-90936v^3+295542v^2+38928v-14333}
                         {536481792}>0 .
\]
Indeed, with \(y=v-1\ge0\) the numerator equals \(y^2(14175y^2-34236y+107784)+413904y+243376\), and the quadratic has negative discriminant. This proves the exclusion over \(\mathbb R\), and the formulas and real inequalities give the same result over every real closed field.


## Exact levels for the special-unitary sign quotients

**Theorem (The universal source torsor gives level four in odd sign).**

Let \(K\) have characteristic zero and let \(X\) be the Hamilton quaternionic isometry torsor from \(I_{n-r}\perp-I_r\) to \(I_n\), with \(r\) odd. Let \(H\) be any reductive subgroup of the special unitary group of the source standard complex plane, and put \(Y=X/H\). Then
\[
                         s(K(Y))=\min(s(K),4).
\]
No generic freeness condition for a second subgroup is needed. In particular the bare full special-unitary quotient in rank three has level four over a real closed field. Over such a field its product with the positive sphere in ambient dimension five has a regular all-order counterexample by the split-plane construction. More generally any pointed compact factor with an active simple factor of absolute rank at least two gives a regular counterexample on the product, by the invariant-fibre criterion.

Let \(F=K(Y)\) and use the fibre of the right \(H\)-torsor \(X\to Y\) at the \(F\)-generic point. Choose a geometric isometry \(g\) in the fibre. The cocycle \(g^{-1}\sigma(g)\) has values in \(H\), hence in the source special unitary group. Twist the standard source complex Hermitian form by this cocycle. Its determinant class in \(F^\times/N(F(i)^\times)\) remains \((-1)^r\) because the twisting maps preserve the exterior volume. On quaternionifying, the twisted form is isometric to the target \(I_n\), since \(g\) intertwines the two descents. If the Hamilton algebra splits over \(F\), its norm represents \(-1\). Otherwise \(F(i)\) is a field, and diagonalizing the descended complex Hermitian form also diagonalizes its quaternionification. The comparison of products of diagonal coefficients under this quaternionic isometry gives \(-1\in \operatorname{Nrd}((-1,-1)_F^\times)\), by the Pfaffian determinant identity used in the two-plane calculation. The use of the complex determinant is valid modulo quaternion reduced norms because complex norms lie among those reduced norms. Thus \(s(F)\le4\).

For the lower bound, if \(s(K)\le4\) take \(K'=K\). Otherwise take \(K'=K(S_4^-)\), the function field of the norm-minus-one quaternion quadric. Its level is four: it has level at most four by the equation, and the four-square form stays anisotropic by Hoffmann separation for the anisotropic five-square projective quadric. Over either choice of \(K'\), the Hamilton norm represents \(-1\), so \(X\) has a \(K'\)-point, obtained by an anti-isometry on each negative line and identity on the other lines. Its image gives a smooth \(K'\)-point of \(Y\). The completion at that point embeds \(K(Y)\) in an iterated Laurent series field over \(K'\). The level of \(K'\) cannot drop there by the leading term of a sum of squares. This proves the lower bound \(\min(s(K),4)\). [Hoffmann, Theorem 1](https://eudml.org/doc/174821)

On the product with the five-dimensional norm-one quadric, choose \(a\in F^4\) with \(\sum a_i^2=-1\). Use the vectors \(e_1=(1,0,0,0,0)\), \(e_2=(0,a_1,a_2,a_3,a_4)\), and multiply both by a common regular denominator. Over a real closed field this denominator can be chosen real on the pointed reference form by multiplying it by its transform under the commuting involution for the sign twist, as in the positive sphere criterion. With \(R=q(e_1)\) after this multiplication and \(U,V\) as in (R1), the regular phase \(\big[(1+U)((1+U)^2V-(2+U)R)\big]^3\) has zero pure moments, and the marker \(U^3\) gives \((3m)!\Lambda_Y(R^{3m})/(5/2)_{3m}\ne0\) at every \(m\ge1\). Every other stated product follows from the level-four criterion for compact factors. These constructions use the original quotient as invariant base.



## Central simple subalgebras and partial index reduction

**Theorem (Central simple embeddings give an exact index-drop test).**

Let \(K\) have characteristic zero, and let \(A,B\) be central simple \(K\)-algebras of the same degree \(n\). On \(X=\operatorname{Isom}_{K\text{-alg}}(A,B)\), let a finite \(K\)-subgroup \(H\) of \(\mathrm{PGL}_1(A)\) act by precomposition. Choose a unital subalgebra \(C\subset B\) that is central simple over its centre \(E\), where \(E/K\) is finite separable of degree \(e\). Put \(m=\deg_E C\), \(r=n/(em)\), and \(D=C_B(C)\), a central simple \(E\)-algebra of degree \(r\). Suppose \(r\ge2\), and write \(h\) for the geometric order of \(H\). Let \(S\) be the image of \(\operatorname{Res}_{E/K}\mathrm{GL}_1(D)\) in the postcomposition group \(\mathrm{PGL}_1(B)\).

The function field \(F=K(X)^S\) is the function field of
\[
          \operatorname{Res}_{E/K}\operatorname{SB}_r
                                      (A_E\otimes_E C^{\mathrm{op}}).
\]
Set \(d=\operatorname{ind}(D_{FE})\), \(F_0=K(X/H)^S\), and \(d_0=\operatorname{ind}(D_{F_0E})\). Then
\[
                         d\mid d_0\mid d\gcd(r/d,h).
\]
On the generic \(S\)-fibre of \(X/H\), the derived group of \(S\) has positive split rank precisely when \(d_0<r\). If so, \(X/H\) has a regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample over \(K\). Thus \(d<r\) is an exact test when \(h\) is prime to \(r\), and \(r/d\nmid h\) suffices for arbitrary \(h\). These criteria apply to \(X/H\) without \(K\)-points.

If \(E/K\) is Galois, for \(\sigma\in\operatorname{Gal}(E/K)\) write \(C_\sigma=C\otimes_{E,\sigma}E\) and \(\mathcal B_\sigma=A_E\otimes_E C_\sigma^{\mathrm{op}}\). Then \(d\) is the gcd of the integers
\[
 \left(\prod_\sigma \frac r{\gcd(r,i_\sigma)}\right)
 \operatorname{ind}\left(D\otimes_E
                         \bigotimes_\sigma\mathcal B_\sigma^{\otimes i_\sigma}\right),
          \qquad 1\le i_\sigma\le nm.
\]
For any finite separable \(E/K\), it suffices that the index of \(D\) drop below \(r\) over the single field \(E(\operatorname{SB}_r(A_E\otimes_E C^{\mathrm{op}}))\).

**Proof.** The centralizer theorem applied first to \(E\subset B\) and then to \(C\subset C_B(E)\) gives the degree of \(D\). The map
\[
                       \varphi\longmapsto \varphi^{-1}|_C
\]
identifies \(X/\!/S\) with the variety of embeddings of \(C\) into \(A\) having multiplicity \(r\) on each geometric simple block. Over an algebraic closure these embeddings form a single conjugacy class whose stabilizer is the corresponding centralizer. The map is its affine homogeneous quotient, with fibres the postcomposition \(S\)-orbits. This identification descends to \(K\).

This embedding variety is the open in the stated Weil restriction on which the evaluation map is invertible. To see this on a splitting field, write \(A=\operatorname{End}(V)\) and \(C=\prod_\sigma\operatorname{End}(W_\sigma)\). An embedding specifies its multiplicity spaces \(U_\sigma=\operatorname{Hom}_C(W_\sigma,V)\) of dimension \(r\), each in \(\operatorname{Hom}(W_\sigma,V)\). These are points of the respective Grassmannians for the degree \(nm\) algebras. Conversely, if
\[
                  \bigoplus_\sigma W_\sigma\otimes U_\sigma
                              \longrightarrow V
\]
is an isomorphism by evaluation, transport the blockwise action to \(V\). These operations are inverse and descend. In right-ideal coordinates the subspace condition is that the image of every endomorphism in the ideal consist of intertwiners. It is expressed by
\((\iota(c)\otimes 1)z=(1\otimes c^{\mathrm{op}})z\) in the corresponding factor, so it is independent of the chosen split models. The open is geometrically nonempty and dense.

When \(E/K\) is Galois, after adjoining \(E\) the Weil restriction is a product of the \(\operatorname{SB}_r(\mathcal B_\sigma)\). Adjoin the function fields of the factors successively and apply the index formula for a generalized Severi–Brauer variety at each step. Since \(r\mid nm\), iteration gives the displayed gcd. For separable \(E/K\) the identity factor in \(E\otimes_K E\) gives a dominant projection over \(E\) to \(\operatorname{SB}_r(A_E\otimes_E C^{\mathrm{op}})\). An index drop over that factor persists over \(FE\). [Karpenko, formula in Lemma III.2](https://sites.ualberta.ca/~karpenko/publ/lnk.pdf)

The generic invariant field for \(X/H\) is \(F_0=F^H\), since the two actions commute. The fields \(FE,F_0E\) are formed from geometrically integral invariant quotients. The degree \([FE:F_0E]\) divides the geometric order of \(H\), as is seen after extending constants to an algebraic closure. Index under extension divides the original index, and the original index divides the product of the extension degree and the new index. In this case \(d\mid d_0\), \(d_0\mid r\), and \(d_0\mid dh\), giving the claimed divisibility. The derived centralizer is a central image of \(\operatorname{Res}_{E/K}\mathrm{SL}_1(D)\). It has positive split rank over \(F_0\) exactly when \(D_{F_0E}\) has matrix size at least two. In that case the scalar operations on two summands give a split rank-one subgroup by restriction of scalars and its diagonal map. Geometric stabilizers of the postcomposition action on \(X/H\) are finite, so that subgroup acts nontrivially on the generic fibre. The generic invariant-fibre theorem then gives one regular phase and one fixed regular multiplier with infinitely many nonzero marked moments. \(\square\)

For a point-free example with both acting groups anisotropic, take \(K=\mathbb Q(i)(u,v,s,t,a,b,c,d_0)\) and the symbol algebras
\[
 \begin{split}
 A&=(u,v)_4\otimes(s,t)_4,\qquad
 C=(a,b)_2\otimes(c,d_0)_2,\\
 D&=(u,v)_2\otimes(s,t)_2,\qquad B=C\otimes_K D .
 \end{split}
\]
Here \(n=16\), \(E=K\), \(m=4\), and \(r=4\). Within each displayed tensor the generators have independent fractional value classes at the coordinate valuations, so \(A,B,C,D\) are division. In the Brauer group \([D]=2[A]\), \(2[C]=0\), and \(A,B\) have periods four and two, respectively. In the index formula write \(\mathcal B=A\otimes C^{\mathrm{op}}\). The term \(i=2\) equals \(2\) because \(D\otimes\mathcal B^{\otimes 2}\) splits. Odd \(i\) give terms divisible by four, \(i\equiv2\bmod 4\) give terms divisible by two, and \(4\mid i\) give \(\operatorname{ind}(D)=4\). Thus the generic index is exactly two. One can take \(H\) to be the \(\ell\)-torsion of a maximal \(K\)-torus of \(\mathrm{PGL}_1(A)\) for any odd prime \(\ell\), giving a nontrivial finite quotient. A \(K\)-point of \(X/H\) would give an \(H\)-torsor of odd degree in \(X\), and hence a point of \(X\) over an extension of odd degree. Restriction to such an extension cannot kill the nonzero \(2\)-primary class \([A]-[B]\), by restriction and corestriction. The counterexample on \(X/H\) uses no split scalar factor.


A quotient by an involution can also retain the index drop. Use \(K=\mathbb Q(\zeta_9)(u,v,s,t,a,b,c,d_0)\) and
\[
 \begin{split}
 A&=(u,v)_9\otimes(s,t)_2,\qquad C=(a,b)_3,\\
 D&=(u,v)_3\otimes(c,d_0)_2,\qquad B=C\otimes_K D .
 \end{split}
\]
Use compatible primitive roots in the symbol convention, so \(3[(u,v)_9]=[(u,v)_3]\). Again all four algebras are division by the independent value classes. Here \(r=6\) and the \(i=6\) term in the index formula is \(2\). The \(3\)-primary class of \(D\otimes (A\otimes C^{\mathrm{op}})^{\otimes 6}\) is zero, and its \(2\)-primary class is the independent quaternion \((c,d_0)_2\). Every term in the gcd is even because the \(2\)-primary class always has the independent \((c,d_0)_2\) component. It follows that \(d=2\). The class of a noncentral symbol generator of \((s,t)_2\) generates an order-two subgroup \(H\) in \(\mathrm{PGL}_1(A)\). The divisibility bound gives \(d_0=2\) since \(r/d=3\). The difference \([A]-[B]\) has \(3\)-primary order nine and so survives every extension of degree at most two. Hence this quotient is point-free and has a regular counterexample without a scalar factor.


## Transverse saddle zeros in the two-angle trace

**Theorem (An imaginary saddle on each maximizing fibre forces nonvanishing).**

Let \(X=\mathrm{SO}(3)/\Gamma\) be the pointed compact form for a finite rotation group, and use the product with a split scalar \(t\). Lift to \(\mathrm{SO}(3)\), write \(x=g^{-1}e\) for the sphere projection for a fixed unit vector \(e\), and use a local left-circle angle \(\psi\) above \(x\). Consider a real regular phase
\[
            P=A(x)+\sum_{j=1}^d B_j(x,\psi)(t^j-t^{-j}).
\]
Here \(A\) is angular and each \(B_j\) is an arbitrary real regular function on \(X\). For \(t=e^{i\theta}\) put
\[
                      V_x(\psi,\theta)=2\sum_j B_j(x,\psi)\sin(j\theta).
\]
At every maximizing \(x_0\) of \(A\), assume that \(\sum_j jB_j(x_0,\psi)\) has a simple zero on the left circle and that every critical point of \(V_{x_0}\) at value zero on its two-torus is a nondegenerate saddle. Then the pure moments of \(P\) are nonzero at arbitrarily large orders. The conclusion also holds with minimizing fibres in the hypotheses. It applies over any real closed field, and in particular to both exceptional products.

**Proof.** First work over \(\mathbb R\). Write \(a=\max A\). The Cauchy transform of the compact comparison trace, at real \(z>a\), is
\[
 I(z)=\int_{\mathbb S^2} J_x(z-A(x))\,d\sigma(x),\qquad
 J_x(p)=\int_{\mathbb T^2}\frac{p}{p^2+V_x^2}\,
                                 \frac{d\psi\,d\theta}{(2\pi)^2}.
\]
This uses the oddness of \(V_x\) in \(\theta\). If the moments vanish eventually, the Cauchy series at infinity is a polynomial in \(1/z\) with coefficient one at \(1/z\). It continues to equal \(I\) on \(z>\max(a,0)\). For \(a<0\), \(I\) extends analytically through \(0\), contradicting that polynomial. For \(a=0\), \(0\le zI(z)\le1\), and dominated convergence gives
\[
                \lim_{z\downarrow 0}zI(z)
                      =\operatorname{Prob}(A=0,\ V=0)<1 .
\]
The simple-zero hypothesis ensures \(V\) is nonzero on a nonempty open set. The bound on \(zI(z)\) forces the putative polynomial to be \(1/z\), which has the wrong limit. It remains to treat \(a>0\).

Fix a maximizing \(x_0\). At the simple zero of \(\sum jB_j(x_0,\psi)\), the point \((\psi,0)\) has \(V=0\) and zero first derivatives. Its Hessian has zero diagonal and nonzero mixed entry \(2\partial_\psi\sum jB_j\). The implicit function theorem gives a saddle of this type with critical value exactly zero throughout a neighborhood of \(x_0\). The other critical zeros at \(x_0\) are finitely many by compactness and nondegeneracy. They continue as saddles with critical values \(c_s(x)\) near zero. In a sufficiently small neighborhood, all critical points at levels sufficiently close to zero lie on these branches, by compactness.

In analytic Morse coordinates at a branch, the function is \(c_s(x)+uv\) on a small square, and the angular measure has analytic density \(\rho_{s,x}(u,v)>0\). Its pushforward near \(c_s\) has density
\[
                 -b_s(x)\log|v-c_s(x)|+E_{s,x}(v),
                 \qquad b_s(x)=2\rho_{s,x}(0,0)>0 ,
\]
where \(v\) now denotes the value variable. The remainder is uniformly Hölder for any fixed exponent \(0<\alpha<1\). For this calculation expand the density into convergent monomials on a square of radius \(\epsilon\).
